# Calculus: master checklist

Source: G. Strang, E. Herman et al., Calculus, Volumes 1-3, OpenStax 2016 (item numbers `V:item`, `V` the volume; `pNNN` the pdf page). Statements are our own one-line paraphrases. Generated by `docs/coverage/calculus/parts/merge.py` from the audit parts; maps are generated by `scripts/coverage/book_maps.py`.

| id | statement | OpenStax Calculus | classification | where | note |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| | **01 Functions** | | | | |
| CA-001 | A function from a set X to a set Y assigns to each input in X exactly one output in Y; the inputs form the domain and the outputs attained form the range. | 1:§1.1 Definition (function) p16 | not a result |  | Basic notion; in Lean it is just a function type (domain = type/subset, range = Set.range). Default-domain convention is part of this row. |
| CA-002 | A zero of f is an input x with f(x)=0; the x-intercepts of the graph are the points (x,0) at the zeros, and the y-intercept is (0,f(0)). | 1:§1.1 Zeros and intercepts p22 | not a result |  | Terminology only. |
| CA-003 | A curve in the plane is the graph of a function exactly when every vertical line meets it at most once. | 1:§1.1 Rule: Vertical Line Test p22 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.vertical_line_test | Graph-level statement; no Mathlib counterpart expected. / Functions.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.vertical_line_test_book |
| CA-004 | Definition of increasing, strictly increasing, decreasing and strictly decreasing on an interval via comparing f(x1) and f(x2) for x1<x2. | 1:§1.1 Definition (increasing/decreasing) p25 | in Mathlib | Monotone, StrictMono, Antitone, StrictAnti, MonotoneOn, StrictMonoOn, AntitoneOn, StrictAntiOn | General form for preorders; the interval version is the On variants. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.increasing_decreasing_defs |
| CA-005 | Sum, difference, product and quotient of functions are defined pointwise (quotient where the denominator is nonzero). | 1:§1.1 Combining Functions Using Mathematical Operations p26 | in Mathlib | Pi.add_apply, Pi.sub_apply, Pi.mul_apply, Pi.div_apply | Definitional pointwise operations on function types. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.pointwise_operations |
| CA-006 | The composite (f∘g)(x)=f(g(x)) is defined on those x in the domain of g whose value lies in the domain of f. | 1:§1.1 Definition (composite function) p27 · 1:§1.1 Key Equations (composition) p108 | in Mathlib | Function.comp, Function.comp_apply | Domain restriction handled by total functions or ∘ on subtypes. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.comp_apply_eq |
| CA-007 | A function is even if f(-x)=f(x) and odd if f(-x)=-f(x) for all x in the domain; even graphs are symmetric about the y-axis, odd graphs about the origin. | 1:§1.1 Definition (even and odd functions) p30 | in Mathlib | Function.Even, Function.Odd, Function.Even.eq, Function.Odd.eq | Graph symmetry statement itself is not formalized. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.even_odd_iff |
| CA-008 | The absolute value \|x\| equals x for x≥0 and -x for x<0. | 1:§1.1 Absolute value function p31 · 1:Example 1.11 p32 · 1:§1.1 Key Equations (absolute value) p108 | in Mathlib | abs_of_nonneg, abs_of_neg | Definition in Mathlib is max(x,-x); these lemmas give the piecewise form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.abs_piecewise |
| CA-009 | The slope of the line through (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) is the ratio of the change in y to the change in x, (y2-y1)/(x2-x1), and a linear function f(x)=mx+b has slope m. | 1:§1.2 Definition (slope) p39 | in Mathlib | slope | Mathlib's slope f a b = (b-a)⁻¹ • (f b - f a) is the general (affine-space) form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.slope_ratio_and_linear |
| CA-010 | Point-slope form y-y0=m(x-x0), slope-intercept form y=mx+b, and standard form ax+by=c (a,b not both zero) describe a line. | 1:§1.2 Definition (equations of a line) p40 · 1:§1.2 Key Equations (lines) p108 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.pointSlope_iff_slopeIntercept, UndergradKB.Calculus.standardForm_iff_slopeIntercept, UndergradKB.Calculus.standardForm_vertical | Analytic geometry of lines; used in Geometry topic. / Functions.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.line_equation_forms |
| CA-011 | A polynomial function is a finite sum of coefficient times power of x; its degree is the top exponent with nonzero coefficient, called leading coefficient; degree 2 and 3 are quadratic and cubic. | 1:§1.2 Polynomial function (definition in text) p42 · 1:§1.2 Key Equations (polynomial function) p108 | in Mathlib | Polynomial, Polynomial.degree, Polynomial.leadingCoeff, Polynomial.eval | Formal polynomials evaluated with Polynomial.eval; functions are obtained by evaluation. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C1.polynomial_eval_as_sum |

| CA-084 | The slope of the secant line through $(a,f(a))$ and $(x,f(x))$ is the difference quotient $\frac{f(x)-f(a)}{x-a}$, or with increment $h$, $\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)}{h}$. | 1:§3.1 Definition (difference quotient, with increment) p.197 | in Mathlib | slope | Mathlib's slope f a b is (f b - f a)/(b - a) as a scalar action; the increment form is the case b = a + h. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C3.difference_quotient_increment |

| CA-158 | For a power x^n (n a positive integer) the limit at +infinity is +infinity; at -infinity it is +infinity for even n and -infinity for odd n; for c x^n the signs follow the sign of c. | 1:§4.6 End behavior of power functions p.370 · 1:Example 4.24 p.371 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atTop_kb, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atBot_even, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_pow_atBot_odd, Filter.tendsto_pow_atTop | atTop case and the constant-multiple case (tendsto_const_mul_pow_atTop) are there; the atBot parity cases follow by composing with negation, no direct lemma located. / EndBehavior.lean (with the c·x^n cases). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.end_behavior_power_functions |
| CA-159 | A polynomial of degree n with leading coefficient a_n!=0 behaves like its leading term a_n x^n as x->+-infinity, so it tends to +-infinity according to degree and sign of a_n. | 1:§4.6 End behavior for polynomial functions p.371 · 1:§4.6 Key Concepts p.441 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.isEquivalent_atTop_lead, Polynomial.isEquivalent_atBot_lead, Polynomial.tendsto_atTop_of_leadingCoeff_nonneg, Polynomial.tendsto_atBot_of_leadingCoeff_nonpos | Asymptotic-equivalence form over a normed ordered field. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.polynomial_asymptotic_leading_term, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.polynomial_tendsto_atTop; atTop and positive-leading-coefficient case only |
| CA-160 | For f=p/q with deg p<deg q, f tends to 0 (horizontal asymptote y=0); with equal degrees f tends to the ratio of leading coefficients; with deg p>deg q f tends to plus or minus infinity. | 1:§4.6 End behavior for rational functions p.372 · 1:§4.6 Summary of rational end behavior p.375 · 1:Example 4.25 p.372 · 1:§4.6 Key Concepts p.441 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.div_tendsto_atTop_zero_of_degree_lt, Polynomial.div_tendsto_atTop_leadingCoeff_div_of_degree_eq, Polynomial.div_tendsto_atTop_of_degree_gt, Polynomial.div_tendsto_atBot_of_degree_gt | Mathlib has atTop and atBot versions; the deg p>deg q case requires sign condition on ratio of leading coefficients (and a '(Q != 0)' variant) which the book handles by cases. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_zero, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_leading_ratio, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atTop_of_degree_gt, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C4.rational_tendsto_atBot_of_degree_gt; x to plus infinity |

| CA-179 | Finite sums are linear: sum of a constant n times is nc, constants factor out, sums and differences split termwise, and a sum from m to n equals the sum from 1 to n minus the sum from 1 to m-1. | 1:§5.1 Rule (properties of sigma notation) p.447 · 2:§1.1 Rule: Properties of Sigma Notation p.15 · 2:Key Equations p.110 | in Mathlib | Finset.sum_const, Finset.mul_sum, Finset.sum_add_distrib, Finset.sum_sub_distrib, Finset.sum_Ico_consecutive | General form over commutative (semi)rings and arbitrary index finsets; the split-at-m property follows from Finset.sum_Ico_consecutive (names via to_additive of the product versions). / Stated for general commutative monoids/rings and Finset sums; book form follows by specialising. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_const, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_const_mul, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_add, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_sub, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sigma_split |

Squares and cubes only via the general Faulhaber/Bernoulli formula sum_range_pow over Q (range n), not as the closed forms n(n+1)(2n+1)/6 and the cube-square identity. / Sum of integers is explicit; squares and cubes only through the general Faulhaber/Bernoulli formula sum_range_pow (over Q), no explicit n(n+1)(2n+1)/6 or (n(n+1)/2)^2 statement. / RiemannSums.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.sums_of_powers_of_integers |
| CA-181 | A partition of [a,b] is a finite increasing list of points from a to b cutting it into subintervals; it is regular when all subintervals have equal width. | 1:§5.1 Definition (partition, regular partition) p.450 · 2:§1.1 Definition (partition) p.18 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.regularPartition, UndergradKB.Calculus.regularPartition_mono, BoxIntegral.Prepartition, BoxIntegral.Prepartition.IsPartition | Only the general n-dimensional box partitions (BoxIntegral), not an interval-specific list of points or the regular partition. / Terminology; Mathlib has general (tagged) partitions of boxes, not named as regular partition of an interval. / RiemannSums.lean (regular partitions). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.regular_partition_def |
| CA-182 | Left-endpoint and right-endpoint approximations L_n and R_n of the area under a graph are the sums of f at left or right endpoints times the width Delta x. | 1:§5.1 Rule (left-endpoint approximation) p.450 · 1:§5.1 Rule (right-endpoint approximation) p.451 · 2:§1.1 Rule: Left-Endpoint Approximation p.18 · 2:Example 1.4 p.20 · 2:§1.1 Rule: Right-Endpoint Approximation p.19 · 2:Example 1.5 p.25 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.leftSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.rightSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_leftSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_rightSum | Notation for particular Riemann sums; no statement to formalize. / Definition of an estimate; no named Mathlib definition (only general tagged-partition Riemann sums). / Same as left-endpoint; no named definition. / RiemannSums.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.endpoint_approximations_converge |
| CA-183 | A Riemann sum for f on a regular partition is the sum of f(c_i) Delta x with tag points c_i chosen anywhere in the i-th subinterval. | 1:§5.1 Definition (Riemann sum) p.456 · 2:§1.1 Definition (Riemann sum) p.24 · 2:Example 1.6 p.26 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.taggedSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_taggedSum, BoxIntegral.integralSum | Mathlib has tagged-prepartition Riemann sums for boxes (any partition, tags, volume as a box-additive map); not the regular-partition form specifically. / Mathlib's integralSum is for tagged prepartitions of boxes in R^n (general form); no regular-partition version. / RiemannSums.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.riemann_sum_def |
| CA-184 | For continuous nonnegative f the area under the graph over [a,b] is defined as the limit of Riemann sums as n tends to infinity. | 1:§5.1 Definition (area under a curve) p.456 · 2:§1.1 Definition (area under a curve) p.24 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_leftSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_rightSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_taggedSum, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral | Mathlib identifies the Lebesgue measure of the region between graphs with the integral (needs integrability on the set); the Riemann-sum limit definition itself is not used. / Mathlib proves the measure of the region under the graph equals the integral (general form with measures); area is not defined as a Riemann-sum limit. / RiemannSums.lean: for continuous f the Riemann sums tend to the integral (area itself is Mathlib's measure). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.area_under_curve_as_limit |
| CA-185 | Choosing the tags at maximum (or minimum) points of each subinterval gives an upper (or lower) sum, an over- (or under-) estimate; for monotone f these are the endpoint sums. | 1:§5.1 Definition (upper sum, lower sum) p.457 · 1:Example 5.5 p.457 · 1:Example 5.6 p.458 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.leftSum_le_integral_of_monotoneOn, UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_le_rightSum_of_monotoneOn, UndergradKB.Calculus.rightSum_le_integral_of_antitoneOn, UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_le_leftSum_of_antitoneOn | Darboux upper and lower sums for the interval integral not found in Mathlib. / RiemannSums.lean: the monotone case (upper and lower sums for general f not defined). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.upper_lower_sums_monotone |
| CA-186 | The definite integral of f on [a,b] is the limit of Riemann sums as n tends to infinity, when it exists, and f is then called integrable; a and b are the limits of integration, f the integrand, x the (dummy) variable. | 1:§5.2 Definition (definite integral) p.464 · 2:§1.2 Definition and Notation (definite integral) p.32 · 2:Example 1.7 p.33 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.taggedSum_error_le, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_taggedSum, intervalIntegral, BoxIntegral.HasIntegral, MeasureTheory.ContinuousOn.hasBoxIntegral, IntervalIntegrable | Mathlib's interval integral is defined via the Lebesgue integral on Ioc a b (with orientation), and IntervalIntegrable is its integrability; the Riemann-sum limit appears as BoxIntegral.HasIntegral with the Riemann filter, in several variables. / Mathlib's interval integral is Lebesgue/Bochner; Riemann-sum characterisation only via BoxIntegral (box integral with Riemann parameters), not a regular-partition limit. / RiemannSums.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.definite_integral_as_limit_of_riemann_sums |
| CA-187 | A function continuous on [a,b] is integrable there, and the Riemann sums converge to a limit independent of the tag choices; with finitely many jump discontinuities f stays integrable. | 1:Theorem 5.1 p.465 · 2:Theorem 1.1 p.33 | in Mathlib | ContinuousOn.intervalIntegrable, Continuous.intervalIntegrable, MeasureTheory.ContinuousOn.hasBoxIntegral, MeasureTheory.AEContinuous.hasBoxIntegral | Interval integrability (Lebesgue sense) is the direct statement; Riemann convergence with arbitrary tags is the BoxIntegral version (n-dimensional general form, a.e. continuous bounded functions also covered). / Integrable in the Lebesgue sense, and Riemann (box integral) sense via hasBoxIntegral; general form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.continuousOn_integrable; interval integrability only; Riemann-sum convergence and the finite-jump case not wrapped |
| CA-188 | The net signed area between f and the x-axis (area above minus area below) equals the definite integral of f, and the total area equals the integral of \|f\|. | 1:§5.2 Definition (net signed area, total area) p.471 · 2:§1.2 Definition (net signed area, total area) p.39 · 2:Example 1.9 p.36 · 2:Example 1.10 p.39 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.net_signed_area, UndergradKB.Calculus.total_area, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral | Area between graphs as an integral is there for the region between two functions; the split into above/below parts and the \|f\| form are not stated as such. / Terminology; net signed area is just the integral, total area is the integral of \|f\| (MeasureTheory has no named notion). / AreasBetween.lean: areas as Lebesgue measure of the regions. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C5.net_signed_and_total_area |

| CA-224 | For integrands with sqrt(a^2 + x^2), substitute x = a tan(theta) (theta in (-pi/2, pi/2)) so the root becomes a sec(theta). | 2:§3.3 Strategy (sqrt(a^2 + x^2)) p.263 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_add_mul_tan, UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_mul_tan, intervalIntegral.integral_comp_mul_deriv, Real.hasDerivAt_arctan, Real.hasDerivAt_tan | Only the general substitution rule and derivatives of arctan/tan; the recipe is not stated. / TrigIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.trig_substitution_tangent |
| CA-225 | For integrands with sqrt(x^2 - a^2), substitute x = a sec(theta) (theta in [0,pi/2) for x >= a, in (pi/2, pi] for x <= -a) so the root becomes a tan(theta) up to sign. | 2:§3.3 Strategy (sqrt(x^2 - a^2)) p.266 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_sub_mul_sec, UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_sub_mul_sec_of_first, UndergradKB.Calculus.sqrt_sq_sub_mul_sec_of_second, UndergradKB.Calculus.hasDerivAt_mul_sec | Mathlib has Real.hasDerivAt_arcosh for the related hyperbolic substitution but no secant-substitution statement. / TrigIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.trig_substitution_secant |
| CA-226 | The antiderivative of 1/sqrt(1+x^2) is ln(x + sqrt(1+x^2)) (equivalently arcsinh x), obtained via x = tan(theta) or x = sinh(theta). | 2:Example 3.24 p.264 · 2:Example 3.25 p.265 | in Mathlib | Real.arsinh, Real.hasDerivAt_arsinh | Special case a = 1 as the book reads (integrand text is stripped in the source); Mathlib defines arsinh x as log(x + sqrt(1+x^2)) and proves its derivative is (sqrt(1+x^2))^(-1). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.hasDerivAt_log_x_add_sqrt; case a=1 |
| CA-227 | A rational function P/Q with deg P >= deg Q is first divided: P/Q = polynomial + remainder/Q with deg remainder < deg Q, then integrated termwise. | 2:§3.4 Requirement: degree of numerator less than denominator p.271 · 2:Example 3.28 p.271 · 2:Example 3.30 p.273 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polynomial_long_division, Polynomial.modByMonic_add_div | Polynomial long division (for monic divisor) is in Mathlib; its use for integrating rational functions is not. / PolynomialDivision.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.proper_fraction_by_long_division |
| CA-228 | Every real polynomial denominator factors into linear factors and irreducible quadratic factors, which determines the shape of the decomposition. | 2:§3.4 Problem-Solving Strategy (Partial Fraction Decomposition), step 2 p.275 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.real_polynomial_factorization, Irreducible.degree_le_two | Mathlib proves real irreducible polynomials have degree at most 2; the full factorization statement is obtained from unique factorization (not checked as a single named theorem). / PolynomialDivision.lean: linear and irreducible quadratic factors. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.real_polynomial_linear_quadratic_factors |
| CA-229 | If Q has distinct linear factors, P/Q (deg P < deg Q) is a sum of constants over each linear factor. | 2:§3.4 Nonrepeated linear factors p.271 · 2:Example 3.29 p.271 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.div_prod_eq_quo_add_sum_rem_div | General form over a field with pairwise coprime monic denominators and remainders of smaller degree; distinct linear factors are coprime monic, so the book statement follows by specialising. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_distinct_linear; existence with polynomial part q; q=0 when deg P < deg Q not proved |
| CA-230 | A repeated linear factor (x-a)^n in Q contributes terms A_1/(x-a) + ... + A_n/(x-a)^n to the decomposition. | 2:§3.4 Repeated linear factors p.274 · 2:Example 3.32 p.274 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.eq_quo_mul_pow_add_sum_rem_mul_pow | General form: expansion of f in powers of a monic g with remainders of degree less than deg g; dividing by g^n gives the book form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_repeated_linear; polynomial identity form (divide by (X-a)^n) |
| CA-231 | Each irreducible quadratic factor ax^2+bx+c of Q contributes a term (Ax+B)/(ax^2+bx+c). | 2:§3.4 Strategy step 3c p.275 · 2:Example 3.33 p.275 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.div_prod_eq_quo_add_sum_rem_div | General form with monic coprime denominators and remainder degree below that of the denominator (degree < 2 gives Ax+B); irreducibility is not required there. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_quadratic_factors; monic quadratics; irreducibility not needed |
| CA-232 | A repeated irreducible quadratic factor (ax^2+bx+c)^n contributes terms (A_k x+B_k)/(ax^2+bx+c)^k for k = 1..n. | 2:§3.4 Strategy step 3d p.275 · 2:Example 3.34 p.276 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.eq_quo_mul_pow_add_sum_rem_mul_pow | General form: powers-of-g expansion with remainder degree below deg g; specialises to the book form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.partial_fractions_repeated_quadratic; polynomial identity form |
| CA-233 | To find decomposition constants, equate coefficients of an identity between polynomials, or substitute chosen values of x, since polynomials equal at all reals are identical. | 2:§3.4 Rule (Method of Equating Coefficients) p.272 · 2:§3.4 Rule (Method of Strategic Substitution) p.272 | in Mathlib | Polynomial.funext, Polynomial.ext | Both rules rest on the identity theorem for polynomials (equality of values at infinitely many points gives equality of polynomials); the recipes themselves are not stated. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.equating_coefficients, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.strategic_substitution |
| CA-234 | The midpoint Riemann sum M_n of a continuous function on [a,b] converges to the integral as n tends to infinity. | 2:Theorem 3.3 p.285 · 2:Example 3.39 p.285 · 2:Example 3.40 p.286 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.midSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_midSum, MeasureTheory.ContinuousOn.hasBoxIntegral | Only general Riemann integrability of continuous functions (box integral, arbitrary tagged partitions); no midpoint-rule definition or equal-width midpoint convergence statement. / RiemannSums.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.midpoint_sum_converges |
| CA-235 | The trapezoidal sum T_n (half the endpoint values plus interior values, times the step) converges to the integral as n tends to infinity. | 2:Theorem 3.4 p.287 · 2:Example 3.41 p.288 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.trapezoidal_integral_eq_avg, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_trapezoid, trapezoidal_integral, trapezoidal_error_le_of_c2 | Mathlib defines the trapezoidal sum and proves an error bound with 1/N^2, giving convergence for C^2 functions; convergence for merely continuous functions is not stated. / RiemannSums.lean (Mathlib's trapezoidal_integral). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.trapezoidal_sum_converges |

| CA-237 | If \|f''\| <= M on [a,b], the midpoint error is at most M(b-a)^3/(24n^2) and the trapezoidal error is at most M(b-a)^3/(12n^2). | 2:Theorem 3.5 p.289 · 2:Example 3.44 p.289 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.midpoint_error_le, UndergradKB.Calculus.trapezoid_error_le, trapezoidal_error_le, trapezoidal_error_le_of_c2 | Trapezoidal bound \|b-a\|^3 ζ/(12 N^2) is in Mathlib (ζ bounds the second derivative); the midpoint bound is not. / RiemannSums.lean: f of class C² on ℝ, \|f''\| ≤ M on [a,b]. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C3.midpoint_trapezoid_error_bounds |

| CA-241 | Improper integrals over [a,inf), (-inf,b], (-inf,inf) are defined as limits of proper integrals (the last by splitting at a point); convergent iff the limits exist. | 2:§3.7 Definition (infinite intervals, Eq. 3.16-3.18) p.296 · 2:Example 3.50 p.300 · 2:Example 3.51 p.300 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_intervalIntegral_of_integrableOn_Ioi, UndergradKB.Calculus.converges_Ioi_iff_integrableOn, UndergradKB.Calculus.converges_of_le_of_converges, UndergradKB.Calculus.diverges_of_le_of_diverges, UndergradKB.Calculus.converges_Ioi_one_rpow_iff, MeasureTheory.intervalIntegral_tendsto_integral_Ioi, MeasureTheory.integrableOn_Ioi_of_intervalIntegral_norm_bounded | Mathlib's integral over Ioi is the Lebesgue integral; it proves the limit of interval integrals equals it when integrable, and integrability from bounded absolute interval integrals. | CA-249 | If f and g are continuous with g(x) <= f(x) on [a,b], the area of the region between their graphs over [a,b] is the integral of f - g. | 1:Theorem 6.1 p.549 · 2:Theorem 2.1 p.117 · 2:§2.1 Key Equation (2.1) p.117 | in Mathlib | volume_regionBetween_eq_integral, regionBetween | General form: product measure of regionBetween f g s for any sigma-finite measure and integrable f,g on a measurable set s with f <= g; area is Lebesgue measure (book does not define area). / General form: product measure mu.prod volume of regionBetween f g s equals ofReal of the integral of g - f over a measurable set s; needs IntegrableOn and f <= g on s (no continuity needed). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves; area as Lebesgue measure of regionBetween |
| CA-250 | For continuous f,g over [a,b] whose graphs may cross, the area between them is the integral of \|f - g\|, computed by splitting at crossings. | 1:Theorem 6.2 p.551 · 2:Theorem 2.2 p.119 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_crossing, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral, volume_regionBetween_eq_lintegral | Follows from the area theorem applied piecewise (regionBetween on subintervals); no lemma stated with \|f-g\|. / Mathlib regionBetween is for one fixed ordering f < g on s; the absolute-value form is not stated, but follows by applying the lemma on the pieces where f <= g and g <= f. / merged rows were classified differently by the chapter audits (not formalized, partly in Mathlib); kept as partly in Mathlib, see the book maps for each volume / AreasBetween.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_crossing_curves |
| CA-251 | If u and v are continuous with v(y) <= u(y) on [c,d], the area between the curves x=v(y), x=u(y) is the integral of u - v over [c,d]. | 1:Theorem 6.3 p.554 · 2:Theorem 2.3 p.122 · 2:§2.1 Key Equation (2.2) p.122 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.area_between_curves_in_y, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral | Mathlib states only the vertical-slab version (functions of the first coordinate); the y-version follows by swapping coordinates (measure-preserving swap) but is not stated. / The lemma is for regions between graphs of functions of the first coordinate; the y-version needs the coordinate swap of the product measure (not stated as such). / AreasBetween.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.area_between_curves_in_y_book |
| CA-252 | A (generalized) cylinder is a plane region translated along the perpendicular axis; its volume is base area times height. | 1:§6.2 Definition (cylinder) and volume formula A*h p.559 · 2:§2.2 Definition (cylinder, cross-section) p.127 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.Measure.prod_prod | General form: measure of a product set s x t equals mu s * nu t; a cylinder is region x interval. / General form: measure of s x t is mu s * nu t, with the cylinder modelled as base x interval. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.cylinder_volume |
| CA-253 | Volume of a solid with cross-sectional area A(x) over [a,b] is the integral of A(x) dx (slicing method). | 1:§6.2 slicing method p.561 · 2:§2.2 Rule: slicing method (volume as integral of cross-section area) p.129 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_eq_lintegral_slices, UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_solid_of_slices, MeasureTheory.Measure.prod_apply | General form (Cavalieri/Tonelli): measure of a measurable set in a product equals the lintegral of the measures of its slices. / General form (Cavalieri/Fubini): measure of a measurable set in a product space is the integral of the measures of its sections; identifying R^3 with R x R^2 is left to the user. / merged rows were classified differently by the chapter audits (in Mathlib, partly in Mathlib); kept as partly in Mathlib, see the book maps for each volume / Volumes.lean: genuine volume (Lebesgue measure on ℝ³). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V1C6.slicing_method |

| | **11 Power series and Taylor series** | | | | |
| CA-360 | A power series centered at a is a series of the form $\sum_{n\ge0} c_n (x-a)^n$ (centered at 0 when a=0); the convention is that the n=0 term is $c_0$ even at x=a. | 2:§6.1 Definition (power series centered at a) p.478 | in Mathlib | FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars, FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalarsSum | General form: Mathlib uses formal multilinear series (coefficients c_n scalar via ofScalars) evaluated at x-a; the centering shift is done with HasFPowerSeriesAt. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_centered_at |
| CA-361 | For a power series exactly one holds: it converges only at the center, or converges for all real x, or there is R>0 with convergence for \|x-a\|<R and divergence for \|x-a\|>R (endpoints undetermined). | 2:Theorem 6.1 p.479 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_convergence_cases, FormalMultilinearSeries.radius, FormalMultilinearSeries.summable_norm_apply, FormalMultilinearSeries.not_summable_norm_of_radius_lt_nnNorm, FormalMultilinearSeries.le_radius_of_summable_norm | Mathlib has the radius (as an extended nonnegative real, covering the three cases), absolute convergence inside it and non-absolute-convergence outside; the packaged statement that the series itself diverges for \|x\|>R (via terms not tending to 0) and the three-way split are not stated as one theorem. Book proof uses least upper bound; Mathlib defines the radius directly. / PowerSeries.lean: radius (ofScalars ℝ c).radius. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_convergence_trichotomy |
| CA-362 | Interval of convergence is the set of x where the series converges; the radius of convergence R is the number such that convergence holds for \|x-a\|<R and divergence for \|x-a\|>R, with R=0 if only at the center and R=infinity if everywhere. | 2:§6.1 Definition (interval and radius of convergence) p.480 | in Mathlib | FormalMultilinearSeries.radius | Mathlib's radius is defined as the supremum of r for which \|c_n\| r^n is bounded (equivalent to the book's); value is in ENNReal so infinity is top. The interval of convergence as a set is not a named object. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_of_convergence; Radius is Mathlib's FormalMultilinearSeries.radius of ofScalars. |
| CA-363 | Radius of convergence is found with the ratio test: if $\|c_n/c_{n+1}\|$ tends to L then R=L (R=0 or infinity for the extreme limits). | 2:Example 6.1 p.481 | in Mathlib | FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_radius_eq_of_tendsto, FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_radius_eq_inv_of_tendsto, FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_radius_eq_top_of_tendsto, FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_radius_eq_zero_of_tendsto | Book only illustrates the technique on three series (not stated as a theorem); Mathlib states it for ofScalars series over a normed field. Endpoint tests are separate computations. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_eq_of_ratio_tendsto, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_eq_top_of_ratio_tendsto_zero, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.radius_eq_zero_of_ratio_tendsto_atTop |
| CA-364 | $1/(1-x)=\sum_{n\ge0}x^n$ for \|x\|<1, and the series diverges for \|x\|>=1; this represents a function by a power series. | 2:§6.1 Geometric series as power series (6.3) p.483 | in Mathlib | Real.one_div_one_sub_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero, hasSum_geometric_of_abs_lt_one | Real.one_div_one_sub_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero gives the power series on the ball of radius 1; divergence for \|x\|>=1 follows from radius computations. | CA-365 | If two power series converge to f and g on a common interval I, then their sum converges to f+g, $\sum c_n b x^{n+m}$ converges to $b x^m f(x)$, and the series with x replaced by $b x^m$ converges to $f(bx^m)$ whenever $bx^m$ is in I. | 2:Theorem 6.2 p.489 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_algebra, HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.add, HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.const_smul, HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.sub, HasFPowerSeriesAt.compContinuousLinearMap, HasSum.add | Sum, difference, scalar multiple are there (general form for formal multilinear series); the multiplication by x^m (shift) and substitution b x^m (not just a linear substitution bx) are not packaged for power series. / PowerSeriesAlgebra.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_sum_and_substitution |
| CA-366 | If $\sum a_nx^n$ and $\sum b_nx^n$ converge to f and g on a common interval, then the Cauchy product with coefficients $\sum_{k\le n}a_kb_{n-k}$ converges to $fg$ there. | 2:Theorem 6.3 p.495 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_product, UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_product_of_lt_radius, tsum_mul_tsum_eq_tsum_sum_antidiagonal_of_summable_norm, AnalyticAt.mul | Mathlib proves the Cauchy product for absolutely summable series (which covers the open interval of convergence) and that a product of analytic functions is analytic; the book's statement as written (any common interval) is true only inside the radius or with absolute convergence, so the endpoint case is not claimed in Mathlib. The power-series-coefficient form of the product is not stated. / PowerSeriesAlgebra.lean: under absolute convergence (the book's any common interval form is not stated). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_cauchy_product |
| CA-367 | $1/(1-x)^2=\sum_{n\ge0}(n+1)x^n$ for \|x\|<1 (diverging at both endpoints), hence $\sum n x^n=x/(1-x)^2$ and for instance $\sum n/2^n=2$. | 2:Example 6.8 p.496 · 2:Example 6.9 p.498 | in Mathlib | Real.one_div_one_sub_sq_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero, tsum_coe_mul_geometric_of_norm_lt_one | Found once by multiplying the geometric series with itself and again by differentiating it; same result. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.one_div_one_sub_sq_power_series, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sum_n_mul_geometric, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.sum_n_div_two_pow |
| CA-368 | A power series with radius R can be differentiated and integrated term by term on \|x-a\|<R, with the same radius; the sums are f' and the antiderivative of f (endpoints behave separately). | 2:Theorem 6.4 p.497 · 2:§6.2 Term-by-term differentiation/integration (terminology) p.497 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_hasDerivAt, UndergradKB.Calculus.power_series_integral_hasDerivAt, HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.hasFDerivAt, HasFPowerSeriesAt.hasDerivAt, HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.fderiv_eq, hasDerivAt_tsum, intervalIntegral.hasSum_intervalIntegral_of_summable_norm | Differentiability of the sum and derivative as a series is in Mathlib (general form, derivative series via changeOrigin); the explicit coefficients $n c_n x^{n-1}$ and $c_n x^{n+1}/(n+1)$ and the equality of radii are not stated; integration exchange is the general form for continuous functions with summable sup norms. / PowerSeriesDeriv.lean: for \|x−a\| < R (equality of the radii not stated). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_term_by_term |
| CA-369 | $\ln(1+x)=\sum_{n\ge1}(-1)^{n+1}x^n/n$ for -1<x<=1 (interval (-1,1] since the series converges at 1 but diverges at -1). | 2:Example 6.10 (part a) p.499 · 2:Table 6.1 (ln(1+x)) p.524 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.alternating_harmonic_sum_eq_log_two, UndergradKB.Calculus.log_one_add_series, UndergradKB.Calculus.log_one_add_series_diverges_at_neg_one, Real.hasSum_pow_div_log_of_abs_lt_one, Real.tendsto_tsum_powerSeries_nhdsWithin_lt | Mathlib has $-\log(1-x)=\sum x^{n+1}/(n+1)$ for \|x\|<1 (substitute -x); the endpoint x=1 (value ln 2) needs Abel's theorem, which Mathlib has but the explicit ln 2 series is not stated. / AlternatingSeries.lean: on (−1, 1] (endpoint 1 by Abel's theorem). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.log_one_add_power_series |
| CA-370 | $\arctan x=\sum_{n\ge0}(-1)^n x^{2n+1}/(2n+1)$ for \|x\|<=1 (endpoints by the alternating series test and Abel's theorem). | 2:Example 6.10 (part b) p.499 · 2:Table 6.1 (arctan x) p.524 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.arctan_series, Complex.hasSum_arctan, Real.tendsto_sum_pi_div_four | Mathlib states the series for the complex arctan on the unit disc (specialise to real x); the endpoint x=1 appears as Leibniz's series for pi/4, x=-1 by oddness not stated separately. / SeriesArctanExp.lean: \|x\| ≤ 1. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.arctan_power_series_interval |
| CA-371 | Abel's theorem (mentioned, not stated or proved): if a power series converges at an endpoint of its interval, the function it defines is continuous up to that endpoint, so the series sums to the limit of the function there. | 2:§6.2 Abel's theorem (mentioned) p.500 | in Mathlib | Real.tendsto_tsum_powerSeries_nhdsWithin_lt, Complex.tendsto_tsum_powerSeries_nhdsWithin_stolzSet | Book only cites it as beyond scope; Mathlib states it at the point 1 (rescale for other endpoints). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.abel_limit_theorem; At the point 1, from the left. |
| CA-372 | If two power series centered at a agree on an open interval around a, then all their coefficients are equal. | 2:Theorem 6.5 p.500 | in Mathlib | HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries, HasFPowerSeriesAt.eq_formalMultilinearSeries_of_eventually, FormalMultilinearSeries.ofScalars_series_injective | General form for formal multilinear series valued in a normed space; scalar-coefficient form via ofScalars injectivity. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.power_series_coefficients_unique |

(appears in AnalyticAt.hasFPowerSeriesAt and AnalyticOn.hasFPowerSeriesOnBall). / TaylorSeries.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_series_definition |

| CA-375 | The nth Taylor polynomial of f at a is $\sum_{k\le n}f^{(k)}(a)(x-a)^k/k!$, the nth partial sum of the Taylor series; at a=0 it is the Maclaurin polynomial. | 2:§6.3 Definition (Taylor and Maclaurin polynomials) p.507 | in Mathlib | taylorWithinEval, taylorWithin | Mathlib's version is taylorWithinEval f n s a x (relative to a set s; take s a neighbourhood or univ). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_polynomial_formula |
| CA-376 | Taylor's theorem with remainder: if f is n+1 times differentiable on an interval containing a and x, then $f(x)-p_n(x)=f^{(n+1)}(c)(x-a)^{n+1}/(n+1)!$ for some c between a and x, hence $\|R_n(x)\|\le M\|x-a\|^{n+1}/(n+1)!$ when $\|f^{(n+1)}\|\le M$. | 2:Theorem 6.7 p.511 · 2:§6.3 Definition (remainder R_n) p.510 | in Mathlib | taylor_mean_remainder_lagrange, taylor_mean_remainder_bound | Lagrange form is exactly the book's; the packaged bound taylor_mean_remainder_bound has n! in the denominator (weaker by a factor n+1), the book's (n+1)! bound follows from the Lagrange form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_theorem_lagrange_remainder, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_remainder_bound; Open interval I, C^(n+1); bound with (n+1)! in the denominator. |
| CA-377 | The Taylor series of 1/x at a=1 is $\sum(-1)^n(x-1)^n$, convergent exactly on (0,2) and converging to 1/x there (geometric series). | 2:Example 6.15 p.515 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.taylorSeriesTerm_inv_at_one, UndergradKB.Calculus.inv_taylor_series_at_one, Real.one_div_sub_hasFPowerSeriesOnBall_zero, HasFPowerSeriesOnBall.comp_sub | Mathlib has the expansion of 1/(r-x) at 0 and recentering lemmas, not this particular expansion at 1. / TaylorSeries.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.taylor_series_of_reciprocal_at_one |

| CA-383 | For a nonnegative integer r, $(1+x)^r=\sum_{n\le r}\binom rn x^n$ with $\binom rn=r!/(n!(r-n)!)$ (finite binomial expansion). | 2:§6.4 Binomial expansion and binomial coefficients (6.6)-(6.7) p.521 | in Mathlib | add_pow, Commute.add_pow, Nat.choose | Recalled from earlier courses; Mathlib states it for commuting elements of a semiring. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C6.binomial_expansion |

| | **12 Parametric and polar** | | | | |
| CA-389 | Parametric equations x=x(t), y=y(t) with x,y continuous on an interval I define a plane curve C (the set of points as t ranges over I), with t called the parameter; the orientation is the direction of motion as t increases. | 2:§7.1 Definition (parametric equations, parametric curve) p.545 · 3:§1.1 Definition (parametric equations, parameter, plane curve) p.17 | not a result |  | Terminology. In Mathlib a parametrised curve is just a continuous map from an interval to the plane; there is no named structure. / Terminology; a parametric curve is just a continuous map from an interval to the plane. |
| CA-390 | The cycloid traced by a point on a wheel of radius a rolling along a line is $x=a(t-\sin t)$, $y=a(1-\cos t)$. | 2:§7.1 Cycloid equations p.551 · 3:§1.1 cycloid parametrization p.23 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_point, UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_velocity | No cycloid in Mathlib. / Mathlib has no cycloid; curtate/prolate variants (point inside/outside wheel) are the same family. / Parametric.lean: coordinate-level kinematics. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.cycloid_equations |
| CA-391 | A circle of radius b rolling inside a circle of radius a traces the hypocycloid $x=(a-b)\cos t+b\cos\frac{a-b}{b}t$, $y=(a-b)\sin t-b\sin\frac{a-b}{b}t$; the ratio a/b governs the number of cusps (rational gives a closed curve with finitely many cusps). | 2:§7.1 Hypocycloid equations p.551 · 3:§1.1 hypocycloid equations p.24 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.hypocycloid_point | No hypocycloid in Mathlib. Cusp-count claim is stated without proof. / Parametric.lean: coordinate-level kinematics. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.hypocycloid_equations |
| CA-392 | If x'(t) and y'(t) exist and x'(t) is not zero, then $dy/dx=(dy/dt)/(dx/dt)$; this gives slopes of tangents and critical points of a parametric curve. | 2:Theorem 7.1 p.560 · 3:Theorem 1.1 p.32 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_deriv, UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_deriv_exists_inverse, HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse, HasDerivAt.comp | Follows from the chain rule plus the derivative of an inverse function (HasDerivAt.comp, HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse), but no packaged parametric statement exists in Mathlib. / Pieces are in Mathlib (derivative of local inverse, chain rule); the quotient formula dy/dx is not stated as a single lemma. / merged rows were classified differently by the chapter audits (not formalized, partly in Mathlib); kept as partly in Mathlib, see the book maps for each volume / Parametric.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_slope |
| CA-393 | The second derivative of a parametric curve is $d^2y/dx^2=\dfrac{\frac{d}{dt}(dy/dx)}{dx/dt}$. | 2:§7.2 Equation (7.2) p.564 · 2:Definition (second derivative as derivative of the first) p.563 · 3:§1.2 Equation 1.2 (second derivative) p.35 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_second_deriv, HasDerivAt.of_local_left_inverse, HasDerivAt.comp | Chain rule consequence; not packaged in Mathlib. / Follows by applying the first-derivative formula twice; no dedicated lemma. / merged rows were classified differently by the chapter audits (not formalized, partly in Mathlib); kept as partly in Mathlib, see the book maps for each volume / Parametric.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_second_derivative |
| CA-394 | For a non-self-intersecting curve x=x(t), y=y(t) on [t1,t2] with x' continuous, the area under the curve is $\int_{t_1}^{t_2}y(t)x'(t)\,dt$. | 2:Theorem 7.2 p.565 · 3:Theorem 1.2 p.37 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_area_integral, UndergradKB.Calculus.parametric_area, intervalIntegral.integral_comp_mul_deriv, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral | Both ingredients (area under a graph as an integral; substitution rule) are in Mathlib; the combined parametric statement is not, and the book assumes the curve is a graph with x increasing. / Only the underlying substitution rule for interval integrals; the area interpretation is not stated. / Parametric.lean: the area as Lebesgue measure. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_area_under_curve |
| CA-395 | The area under one arch of the cycloid $x=t-\sin t$, $y=1-\cos t$ (0<=t<=2*pi) is $3\pi$. | 2:Example 7.7 p.566 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_arch_integral, UndergradKB.Calculus.cycloid_arch_area | Specific integral of $(1-\cos t)^2$ over a period; the integral itself could be computed with Mathlib's integrals of cos powers but the cycloid statement is not there. / Parametric.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.cycloid_arch_area_example |
| CA-396 | The arc length of the curve x=x(t), y=y(t), a<=t<=b, with x', y' continuous is $\int_a^b\sqrt{x'(t)^2+y'(t)^2}\,dt$ (reducing to $\int\sqrt{1+f'(x)^2}dx$ for y=f(x)). | 2:Theorem 7.3 p.567 · 3:Theorem 1.3 p.39 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parametricArcLength, UndergradKB.Calculus.parametricArcLength_graph | Mathlib has variation of a function (eVariationOn) but no integral formula for curve length. / Mathlib has variation (eVariationOn) but no integral formula for length of a C^1 curve. / Parametric.lean: the integral as the bridging definition (for graphs it is the least upper bound of polygon lengths, ArcLength.lean). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_arc_length |
| CA-397 | Revolving the curve x=x(t), y=y(t) (y>=0) about the x-axis gives surface area $2\pi\int y(t)\sqrt{x'(t)^2+y'(t)^2}\,dt$, and about the y-axis $2\pi\int x(t)\sqrt{x'(t)^2+y'(t)^2}\,dt$ (x>=0). | 2:§7.2 Equation (7.6) p.571 · 3:§1.2 Equation 1.6 (surface area) p.43 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceAreaAboutXAxis, UndergradKB.Calculus.surfaceAreaAboutYAxis | The book applies it to the sphere (Example 7.9, 4*pi*r^2); no surface-area formulas for surfaces of revolution in Mathlib. / Parametric.lean (definition-level). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parametric_surface_area_of_revolution |
| CA-398 | The surface area of a sphere of radius r is 4 pi r^2. | 3:Example 1.9 p.43 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.sphere_surface_area | Mathlib has volume of balls but no surface-area formula. / Parametric.lean (from the surface-of-revolution formula). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.sphere_surface_area_parametric |
| CA-399 | A point is located in polar coordinates (r,theta) by its radial coordinate r (directed distance from the pole) and angular coordinate theta (angle from the polar axis, counterclockwise positive); the pole is the origin and the polar axis is the positive x-axis. | 2:§7.3 Definition (polar coordinate system) p.575 · 3:§1.3 definition of polar coordinates p.47 | in Mathlib | polarCoord, Complex.polarCoord, Complex.arg | General form: polarCoord is an open partial homeomorphism from the plane minus the nonpositive x-axis to (0,infinity) x (-pi,pi), with r>0 and theta from Complex.arg; the book allows any theta and negative r. / Terminology (Mathlib has Complex.arg and Complex.polarCoord as the formal analogue). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_to_rectangular_point |
| CA-400 | Polar and rectangular coordinates are related by $x=r\cos\theta$, $y=r\sin\theta$ and $r^2=x^2+y^2$, $\tan\theta=y/x$ (for x nonzero). | 2:Theorem 7.4 p.575 · 3:Theorem 1.4 p.47 | in Mathlib | Complex.norm_mul_cos_arg, Complex.norm_mul_sin_arg, Complex.tan_arg, polarCoord, Complex.sq_norm | Stated through the complex plane (norm and argument); r^2=x^2+y^2 is the definition of the norm (and appears in polarCoord). / Stated for complex numbers via norm and arg (general form for z in C). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_x_eq_r_cos, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rectangular_y_eq_r_sin, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_radius_sq, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.tan_polar_angle |
| CA-401 | A point has infinitely many polar representations: $(r,\theta)$, $(r,\theta+2\pi n)$ and $(-r,\theta+\pi)$ all give the same point, whereas rectangular coordinates are unique. | 2:§7.3 Non-uniqueness of polar coordinates p.577 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polarPoint_add_int_mul_two_pi, UndergradKB.Calculus.polarPoint_neg_add_pi, UndergradKB.Calculus.polarPoint_eq_iff, Complex.arg_mul_cos_add_sin_mul_I_eq_toIocMod, Complex.arg_mul_cos_add_sin_mul_I_coe_angle | Mathlib has the argument modulo 2*pi for positive r; the negative-radius representation is not stated. / Polar.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_coordinates_non_unique |
| CA-402 | In polar coordinates the equation $\theta=\alpha$ is a line through the pole with slope $\tan\alpha$, and $r=k$ (k>0) is the circle of radius k centred at the origin. | 2:Example 7.13 p.580 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_theta_eq_line, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_r_eq_circle | General statements drawn from Example 7.13(a),(b); trivial consequences of Theorem 7.4. / Polar.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_line_and_circle |
| CA-403 | The rose $r=a\cos n\theta$ (or $a\sin n\theta$) has n petals when n is odd and 2n petals when n is even; non-integer n gives closed curves iff n is rational. | 2:§7.3 Remark on petals of roses p.584 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.rose_petal_count, UndergradKB.Calculus.rose_periodic_iff_rational, UndergradKB.Calculus.rose_period | Stated without proof; the book encourages the reader to explore it. / RosePetals.lean: petals counted by their tips (points of the curve at the maximal distance \|a\|): n tips for odd n, 2n for even n; for real n > 0 the curve is closed (periodic) iff n is rational. Polar.lean: periodicity. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.rose_petals |
| CA-404 | An Archimedean spiral is the polar curve $r=a+b\theta$ (distance proportional to the angle, $r=k\theta$ from the origin), and a logarithmic spiral is a curve with r exponential in theta, the shape of a nautilus shell. | 2:Example 7.14 p.584 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.archimedeanSpiral, UndergradKB.Calculus.logSpiral, UndergradKB.Calculus.spiral_per_turn | Definitions of two spiral families; equation of the logarithmic spiral is given in the book as an exponential in theta. / Polar.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.spirals_example |
| CA-405 | A polar curve $r=f(\theta)$ is parametrised in rectangular coordinates by $x=f(\theta)\cos\theta$, $y=f(\theta)\sin\theta$ with theta as parameter. | 2:§7.3 Polar curve as parametric curve p.586 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polarCurve_range, polarCoord | Mathlib has the map (r,theta) to (r cos theta, r sin theta) as polarCoord.symm; the parametrisation of a curve r=f(theta) is not stated. / Polar.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_curve_as_parametric |
| CA-406 | Symmetry tests for polar curves: about the polar axis if (r,theta) in graph implies (r,-theta) (or (-r,pi-theta)); about the pole if (r,theta) implies (-r,theta) (or (r,theta+pi)); about the vertical line theta=pi/2 if (r,theta) implies (r,pi-theta) (or (-r,-theta)). | 2:Theorem 7.5 p.586 · 3:Theorem 1.5 p.58 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_axis, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_pole, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_symm_vertical, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_curve_symmetry | Geometric symmetry criteria; nothing like it in Mathlib. / Plane geometry fact; sufficient conditions only. / Polar.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_symmetry_tests |
| CA-407 | If f is continuous and nonnegative on [alpha,beta] (beta-alpha<=2*pi), the region between the origin and $r=f(\theta)$ has area $\tfrac12\int_\alpha^\beta f(\theta)^2d\theta$; the area between two polar curves is the difference of such integrals over the common angular range (check the pole as an extra intersection point). | 2:Theorem 7.6 p.593 · 2:Example 7.16 p.593 · 2:Example 7.17 p.594 · 2:§7.4 Intersection at the pole when comparing polar curves p.595 · 2:§7.4 Key concept (area between polar curves) p.622 · 3:Theorem 1.6 p.65 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_region_area, integral_comp_polarCoord_symm, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral, Complex.integral_comp_polarCoord_symm | Mathlib has the change of variables to polar coordinates (integral of r times f(r cos, r sin)); the sector-area formula is the radial integral of r (Fubini) but is not stated. Examples 7.16 (area of a rose petal) and 7.17 (between a circle and a cardioid) are computations. / Mathlib has the polar change-of-variables (Jacobian r) for integrals over C; the area formula itself is not stated. / Polar.lean: the area as Lebesgue measure (polar change of variables). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_region_area_formula |
| CA-408 | For a polar curve $r=f(\theta)$ with f' continuous on [alpha,beta], the arc length is $\int_\alpha^\beta\sqrt{f(\theta)^2+f'(\theta)^2}\,d\theta$. | 2:Theorem 7.7 p.596 · 3:Theorem 1.7 p.68 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_arc_length_integrand, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_arc_length | Special case of the parametric arc length formula (v2c7-parametric-arc-length); no curve length integral in Mathlib. / Polar.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_arc_length_formula |
| CA-409 | Plane sections of a double cone: a plane meeting both nappes gives a hyperbola, a plane parallel to a generating line gives a parabola, a plane perpendicular to the axis gives a circle, and any other plane meeting one nappe gives an ellipse. | 2:§7.5 Conic sections as plane sections of a cone p.600 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_projection, UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_ellipse, UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_parabola, UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_hyperbola, UndergradKB.Calculus.ConeSections.cone_section_both_nappes | Stated geometrically with figures; no conic sections in Mathlib. / ConeSections.lean: planes z = mx + d (d ≠ 0) through the cone x² + y² = z². / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_sections_of_cone |
| CA-410 | A parabola is the set of points equidistant from a fixed point (the focus) and a fixed line (the directrix); the vertex is the point halfway between them. | 2:§7.5 Definition (parabola) p.600 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_vertex_midpoint | Mathlib has no conic sections. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.parabola_vertex_halfway |
| CA-411 | A parabola is the set of points equidistant from a focus and directrix; with vertex (h,k) and focal distance p its standard equation is (y-k)^2=4p(x-h) or (x-h)^2=4p(y-k). | 3:§1.5 Definition (parabola) p.72 · 3:Theorem 1.8 p.73 · 2:Theorem 7.8 p.601 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_horizontal, UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_vertical | Mathlib has no conic sections. / Standard form of a parabola; no conics in Mathlib. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.parabola_standard_equation |
| CA-412 | Reflective properties: rays parallel to the axis of a parabola reflect through the focus; rays from one focus of an ellipse reflect through the other; rays aimed at one focus of a hyperbola reflect toward the other. | 2:§7.5 Reflective properties of parabola, ellipse, hyperbola p.603 · 2:§7.5 Reflective property of ellipse p.607 · 2:§7.5 Reflective property of hyperbola p.611 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_reflective, UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_reflective, UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_reflective | Stated without proof, with applications (dishes, whispering galleries, telescope mirrors). / Conics.lean (parabola), ConicReflection.lean (ellipse and hyperbola): the ray reflected in the tangent line is an explicit positive multiple of the vector to the focus. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_parabola, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_ellipse, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.reflective_property_hyperbola |
| CA-413 | An ellipse is the set of points whose distances to two fixed points (the foci) have a constant sum; the vertices are the ends of the major axis, and the minor axis is perpendicular to it. | 2:§7.5 Definition (ellipse) p.604 · 2:§7.5 Ellipse vertices and axes p.605 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard, UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical | Mathlib has no ellipses. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.ellipse_standard_equation |
| CA-414 | An ellipse (constant sum of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2+(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with foci at distance c, c^2=a^2-b^2. | 3:§1.5 Definition (ellipse) p.76 · 3:Theorem 1.9 p.78 · 2:Theorem 7.9 p.606 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard, UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_standard_vertical | Standard form of an ellipse; no conics in Mathlib. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.ellipse_standard_equation |
| CA-415 | A hyperbola is the set of points whose distances to two fixed points (the foci) differ by a constant (in absolute value). | 2:§7.5 Definition (hyperbola) p.608 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_standard | Mathlib has no hyperbolas. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.hyperbola_standard_equation |
| CA-416 | A hyperbola (constant difference of distances to two foci) has standard form (x-h)^2/a^2-(y-k)^2/b^2=1 with c^2=a^2+b^2, asymptotes y-k=±(b/a)(x-h). | 3:§1.5 Definition (hyperbola) p.80 · 3:Theorem 1.10 p.81 · 2:Theorem 7.10 p.609 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_standard, UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_asymptote | Standard form of a hyperbola; no conics in Mathlib. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.hyperbola_standard_equation |
| CA-417 | Eccentricity e is the ratio of the distance to a focus to the distance to the nearest directrix (e<1 ellipse, e=1 parabola, e>1 hyperbola); focal parameter p is the focus-directrix distance. | 3:§1.5 Definition (eccentricity) p.83 · 3:§1.5 Definition (focal parameter) p.84 · 3:§1.5 Table 1.1 p.85 · 2:§7.5 Definition (eccentricity) p.611 · 2:§7.5 Definition (focal parameter) p.612 · 2:Table 7.1 p.613 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parabola_eccentricity_one, UndergradKB.Calculus.focus_directrix_quadratic, UndergradKB.Calculus.eccentricity_discriminant | Constancy of the ratio and the e-classification are stated as part of the definition box. / Table 7.1 gives e and p for the three conics. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C1.eccentricity_focus_directrix |
| CA-418 | For an ellipse or hyperbola with semi-axis a and focal distance c, the eccentricity is $e=c/a$ (below 1 for the ellipse since c<a, above 1 for the hyperbola since c>a); a parabola has e=1. | 2:§7.5 Eccentricity of ellipse and hyperbola p.612 · 2:Example 7.22 p.612 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_directrix, UndergradKB.Calculus.hyperbola_directrix | Derived in the text from the directrix positions. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_eccentricity |
| CA-419 | A conic with a focus at the pole, eccentricity e and focal parameter p has polar equation $r=\dfrac{ep}{1\pm e\cos\theta}$ (horizontal axis) or $r=\dfrac{ep}{1\pm e\sin\theta}$ (vertical axis). | 2:Theorem 7.11 p.613 · 3:Theorem 1.11 p.85 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_cos_plus, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_cos_minus, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_sin_plus, UndergradKB.Calculus.polar_conic_sin_minus | Book states it without proof ("it is possible to prove"). / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.polar_equation_of_conic |
| CA-420 | The graph of $Ax^2+Bxy+Cy^2+Dx+Ey+F=0$ is a conic: an ellipse if the discriminant $B^2-4AC<0$, a parabola if it equals 0, a hyperbola if it is >0. | 2:§7.5 General equations of degree two (discriminant) p.614 · 3:§1.5 discriminant classification p.86 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_discriminant, UndergradKB.Calculus.discriminant_classification | Degenerate conics are not discussed in the book. / Nondegenerate case only. / Conics.lean: the discriminant is invariant under rotation and its sign is opposite to that of A'C' once B' = 0 (degenerate cases not treated). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.general_quadratic_discriminant |
| CA-421 | When B is nonzero, rotating the axes by an angle theta with $\cot2\theta=(A-C)/B$ removes the cross term, with explicit new coefficients A', B', C', D', E', F'. | 2:§7.5 Rotation of axes for a conic p.615 · 2:Example 7.24 p.615 · 3:§1.5 rotation of axes formulas p.87 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.rotation_equation, UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq, UndergradKB.Calculus.rotB_eq_zero_of_cot, UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_rotB_eq_zero | Rotation formulas are stated without derivation. / Conics.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V2C7.conic_rotation_of_axes |

| CA-449 | The cross products of standard unit vectors: i x j = k, j x k = i, k x i = j, reverse order gives negatives, and each unit vector crossed with itself is zero. | 3:§2.4 text (cross products of standard unit vectors) p.160 · 3:Example 2.33 p.160 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_unit_vectors | No named lemma; each identity follows by evaluating cross_apply (cross_self covers the zero cases). / Vectors.lean (vectors as Fin 3 → ℝ, Mathlib's crossProduct). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_products_of_standard_unit_vectors |
| CA-450 | The cross product is anticommutative, bilinear (distributes over sums, scalars factor out), u x 0 = 0, v x v = 0, and u dot (v x w) = (u x v) dot w. | 3:Theorem 2.6 p.161 · 3:Example 2.32 p.159 · 3:Example 2.34 p.162 | in Mathlib | cross_anticomm, crossProduct, map_add, map_smul, cross_self, triple_product_permutation | crossProduct is a bilinear map (LinearMap), so linearity properties come from the general map lemmas; the last property follows from triple_product_permutation with dotProduct_comm. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_properties |
| CA-451 | The magnitude of u cross v equals \|u\|\|v\| sin theta, where theta is the angle between u and v. | 3:Theorem 2.7 p.162 · 3:Example 2.35 p.162 | in Mathlib | InnerProductGeometry.norm_ofLp_crossProduct, InnerProductGeometry.norm_toLp_symm_crossProduct | Stated for EuclideanSpace R (Fin 3) and for Fin 3 -> R with the L2 norm. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_norm |
| CA-452 | A 2x2 determinant is ad - bc and a 3x3 determinant expands along its first row as a signed sum of 2x2 minors. | 3:§2.4 text (determinants, Eq 2.10) p.163 · 3:Example 2.36 p.163 | in Mathlib | Matrix.det_fin_two, Matrix.det_fin_three | Explicit formulas for 2x2 and 3x3 determinants over a commutative ring; the first-row expansion form is a regrouping (general Laplace expansion also exists). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.det_two_by_two, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.det_three_by_three |
| CA-453 | The cross product is computed as the formal determinant with i, j, k in the first row and the components of the factors in the second and third rows. | 3:§2.4 Rule: Cross Product Calculated by a Determinant p.164 · 3:Example 2.37 p.164 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_apply_eq_det, UndergradKB.Calculus.cross_eq_cofactor | Mnemonic with vectors as matrix entries; its content is the expansion of cross_apply along the first row. / Vectors.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.cross_product_by_determinant |
| CA-454 | The area of the parallelogram with adjacent sides u and v is \|u x v\|; a triangle on them has half that area. | 3:Theorem 2.8 p.165 · 3:Example 2.39 p.165 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.parallelogram_area, InnerProductGeometry.norm_ofLp_crossProduct | Gives \|u x v\| = \|u\|\|v\| sin theta (base times height), but there is no declaration stating the planar area of a parallelogram in R^3. / Vectors.lean (Lagrange identity). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.area_of_parallelogram |
| CA-455 | The triple scalar product u dot (v x w) equals the determinant of the 3x3 matrix with rows u, v, w. | 3:§2.4 Definition (triple scalar product) p.166 · 3:Theorem 2.9 p.166 · 3:Example 2.40 p.167 | in Mathlib | triple_product_eq_det | Over any commutative ring. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.triple_scalar_product_eq_det |
| CA-456 | The triple scalar product is unchanged by cyclic permutation of its three vectors and changes sign when two are swapped. | 3:§2.4 text (identities for permuting a triple scalar product) p.168 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.triple_cyclic, UndergradKB.Calculus.triple_swap, triple_product_permutation, cross_anticomm, Matrix.det_permute | Cyclic invariance is stated; the sign change on a swap follows from cross_anticomm or determinant row swap, not a single lemma. / Vectors.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.triple_scalar_product_permutations |
| CA-457 | The volume of the parallelepiped with edge vectors u, v, w is the absolute value of the triple scalar product. | 3:Theorem 2.10 p.168 · 3:Example 2.41 p.169 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.volume_parallelepiped_triple, MeasureTheory.Measure.addHaar_parallelepiped, triple_product_eq_det | Mathlib: Haar measure of parallelepiped(v) equals \|det of v relative to the basis\|; the Lebesgue volume on R^3 equals the standard basis Haar measure is available but the triple-product statement is not assembled. / Vectors.lean: Lebesgue measure of the parallelepiped. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.volume_of_parallelepiped |
| CA-458 | Three vectors are coplanar if and only if their triple scalar product is zero. | 3:Example 2.42 p.169 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.coplanar_iff_triple_eq_zero, triple_product_eq_det | The determinant form is there; the equivalence of zero determinant with linear dependence (coplanarity) is available through general determinant results, no stated lemma for this. / Vectors.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.coplanar_vectors_triple_product_zero |
| CA-459 | Torque of a force F applied at position vector r from the axis is r cross F, of magnitude \|r\|\|F\| sin theta. | 3:§2.4 Definition (torque) p.170 · 3:Example 2.44 p.171 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.torque_magnitude | Physical definition; cross product itself is in Mathlib. / Vectors.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.torque_cross_product_magnitude |
| CA-460 | The line through P0 with direction vector v has vector equation r = r0 + t v, parametric equations x=x0+ta, y=y0+tb, z=z0+tc and (all of a,b,c nonzero) symmetric equations. | 3:§2.5 text (vector equation of a line, Eq 2.12) p.178 · 3:Theorem 2.11 p.179 · 3:Example 2.45 p.179 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.line_forms, mem_affineSpan_pair_iff_exists_lineMap_eq, AffineMap.lineMap_apply | Mathlib: a point lies on the line through two points iff it is lineMap of a parameter, with lineMap p0 p1 c = c*(p1 - p0) + p0; symmetric form not present. / LinesPlanes.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.line_parametric_and_symmetric |
| CA-461 | The segment between points P and Q is r = (1-t) r_P + t r_Q for t in [0,1], with the corresponding componentwise parametric equations. | 3:§2.5 text (line segment vector equation, Eq 2.15) p.180 · 3:§2.5 text (parametric equations of a segment, Eq 2.16) p.180 · 3:Example 2.46 p.180 | in Mathlib | segment_eq_image_lineMap, AffineMap.lineMap_apply | General form: segment equals the image of the unit interval under lineMap. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.segment_vector_equation |
| CA-462 | The distance from a point Q to the line through P with direction v is \|PQ x v\|/\|v\|; distance is the length of the perpendicular segment. | 3:§2.5 text (distance from a point to a line, definition) p.180 · 3:Theorem 2.12 p.181 · 3:Example 2.47 p.181 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_line | Mathlib has the distance to an affine subspace via orthogonal projection (EuclideanGeometry.dist_orthogonalProjection_eq_infDist) but no cross-product formula. / LinesPlanes.lean: the formula is the attained minimum distance. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_line |
| CA-463 | Two lines in space are equal, parallel and distinct, intersecting in a point, or skew (not parallel and not meeting), decided by direction vectors and a common point. | 3:§2.5 text (skew lines and relationships between lines) p.182 · 3:Example 2.48 p.183 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_classification, UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_classification_exclusive, UndergradKB.Calculus.lines_skew_iff | No affine skew-lines notion found in Mathlib; AffineSubspace.Parallel exists. / LineRelations.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.lines_in_space_relationships |
| CA-464 | The plane through P0 with normal n is the set of points P with n dot (P - P0) = 0, i.e. a(x-x0)+b(y-y0)+c(z-z0)=0, or ax+by+cz+d=0 in general form. | 3:§2.5 Definition (vector and scalar equation of a plane, Eq 2.17, 2.18) p.186 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.plane_forms, UndergradKB.Calculus.planeGen_eq_plane, AffineSubspace.mk', AffineSubspace.mem_mk', Submodule.mem_orthogonal_singleton_iff_inner_right | Mathlib: the affine subspace through p with direction the orthogonal complement of span n has membership q - p in that complement, equivalent to inner n (q - p) = 0; the coordinate (general form) equation is not stated. / LinesPlanes.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.plane_equation_forms |
| CA-465 | The distance from a point to the plane with normal n through Q is \|n dot QP\|/\|n\|, which is \|a x0+b y0+c z0+d\|/sqrt(a^2+b^2+c^2) for the plane ax+by+cz+d=0; parallel planes have constant distance given by this formula. | 3:Theorem 2.13 p.188 · 3:Theorem 2.14 p.191 · 3:Example 2.51 p.188 · 3:Example 2.54 p.192 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane, UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_point_plane_pointNormal, UndergradKB.Calculus.distance_parallel_planes, AffineSubspace.signedInfDist, AffineSubspace.signedInfDist_apply_self, AffineSubspace.abs_signedInfDist_eq_dist_of_mem_affineSpan_insert | Mathlib has signed distance to a hyperplane through orthogonal projection but not the explicit coordinate formula. / LinesPlanes.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.distance_from_point_to_plane |
| CA-466 | Two distinct planes in space are either parallel (parallel normals) or meet in a line; the line of intersection is found by solving the two plane equations. | 3:§2.5 text (parallel and intersecting planes) p.189 · 3:Example 2.52 p.189 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.planes_parallel_or_line | Prose fact used for computation; general affine intersection results exist but no statement for planes in R^3. / LineRelations.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C2.planes_parallel_or_meet_in_line |
