| CA-501 | Without air resistance the horizontal range at fixed speed is maximised by a 45 degree launch angle. | 3:§3.4 range remark p.293 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.projectile_range_formula, UndergradKB.Calculus.range_max_at_pi_div_four | SpaceCurves.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.projectile_range_maximal |
| CA-502 | Kepler: planetary orbits are ellipses with the Sun at a focus; equal areas are swept in equal times; the squares of periods are proportional to the cubes of semimajor axes. | 3:Theorem 3.9 p.293 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_polar_equation, UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_LRL_const, UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_orbit_bounded, UndergradKB.Calculus.angularMomentum_const, UndergradKB.Calculus.keplerSecond_area, UndergradKB.Calculus.orbit_planar | Book sketches proof of the first law. / Kepler.lean, SpaceCurves.lean: first law as the polar equation of a conic with focus at the origin, r(1 + e cos θ) = p (Laplace–Runge–Lenz vector), bounded orbit for e < 1; second law as constant areal rate; planarity. The third law (period) is not proved: it needs the area of the ellipse swept in one period. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.kepler_laws |
| CA-503 | Newton's form of Kepler's third law: P^2 = 4 pi^2 a^3 / (G (M+m)) for a body of mass m orbiting mass M. | 3:§3.4 Equation 3.30 p.295 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.kepler_third_law, UndergradKB.Calculus.ellipse_focal_polar_area, UndergradKB.Calculus.integral_inv_one_add_mul_cos_sq | KeplerThird.lean: a body moving along the focal ellipse r = p/(1+e cos θ) with the law of equal areas r²θ' = h and p = h²/(G(M+m)) takes a time P per revolution with P² = 4π²a³/(G(M+m)), a the semimajor axis (the book's "length of the major axis" is the semimajor axis in the formula); the swept area is πab by the polar area formula. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C3.keplers_third_law |
| | **15 Differentiation of several variables** | | | | |
| CA-504 | A function of two variables assigns to each pair in a subset D of the plane exactly one real number; D is its domain and the set of attained values is its range. | 3:§4.1 Definition (function of two variables) p.310 | not a result |  | Terminology: an ordinary function ℝ × ℝ → ℝ (or on a subset) in Mathlib. |
| CA-505 | The graph of a function of two variables is the set of triples (x,y,f(x,y)) in space, called a surface. | 3:§4.1 Definition (graph of a function of two variables, surface) p.312 | not a result |  | Terminology (graph as a subset of ℝ³); no Mathlib notion of surface needed. |
| CA-506 | The level curve of f at height c is the set of points (x,y) with f(x,y)=c; a collection of level curves is a contour map. | 3:§4.1 Definition (level curve, contour map) p.316 | not a result |  | Terminology: a level set, i.e. the preimage f ⁻¹' {c}; no dedicated Mathlib name. |
| CA-507 | A vertical trace of f is the curve obtained by fixing x=a or y=b in the graph of z=f(x,y). | 3:§4.1 Definition (vertical trace) p.318 | not a result |  | Terminology (restriction of f to a line); no Mathlib name. |
| CA-508 | The level surface of a function of three variables at value c is the set of points (x,y,z) with f(x,y,z)=c. | 3:§4.1 Definition (level surface) p.321 | not a result |  | Terminology: preimage f ⁻¹' {c}; no dedicated Mathlib name. |
| CA-509 | The open disk (plane) or open ball (space, higher dimensions) of radius delta about a point is the set of points at distance less than delta from it. | 3:§4.2 Definition (disk) p.325 · 3:§4.2 Definition (ball in three or more dimensions) p.335 | in Mathlib | Metric.ball | Metric.ball in a metric space; general form (any metric space, e.g. EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n)); on ℝ × ℝ the Mathlib norm is the sup norm so its balls are squares, use EuclideanSpace for round disks. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.disk_eq_dist_lt, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.ball_eq_dist_lt |
| CA-510 | The limit of f at an interior point (a,b) of the domain equals L if for every epsilon there is a delta so that points in the delta-disk around (a,b), other than (a,b), have f within epsilon of L; same definition with balls for three or more variables. | 3:§4.2 Definition (limit of a function of two variables) p.325 | in Mathlib | Metric.tendsto_nhds, Filter.Tendsto | Filter.Tendsto f (nhdsWithin p {p}ᶜ) (nhds L) with Metric.tendsto_nhdsWithin_nhds giving the epsilon-delta form; general form (metric spaces). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_two_variables_iff, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_three_variables_iff |
| CA-511 | The limit of f at a boundary point of the domain is defined with epsilon-delta using only the domain points in a small disk around the point. | 3:§4.2 Definition (limit at a boundary point) p.331 | in Mathlib | Metric.tendsto_nhdsWithin_nhds | Tendsto along nhdsWithin the domain; general form (filters); Metric.tendsto_nhdsWithin_nhds is the epsilon-delta form for a set. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_at_boundary_point_iff |
| CA-512 | Limit laws for functions of two variables: constant, identity, sum, difference, constant multiple, product, quotient (nonzero limit of denominator), positive-integer power, and root laws; they also hold for three or more variables. | 3:Theorem 4.1 p.326 | in Mathlib | Filter.Tendsto.add, Filter.Tendsto.sub, Filter.Tendsto.mul, Filter.Tendsto.const_mul, Filter.Tendsto.div, Filter.Tendsto.pow, tendsto_const_nhds, Filter.Tendsto.comp | General form (any filter, topological semiring/field); identity laws are continuity of the coordinate projections; root law follows from Tendsto.comp with a continuous root function. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_laws, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_const_and_coordinate; plane only; root law not stated |
| CA-513 | If f has limit L at a point, then its restriction along any path or line approaching the point has the same limit, so two paths giving different values show the limit does not exist. | 3:§4.2 Principle (limit fails if path-dependent) p.328 · 3:Example 4.9 p.328 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.not_limit_of_two_paths, UndergradKB.Calculus.no_limit_xy_over_sq_sum, Filter.Tendsto.comp, tendsto_nhds_unique | The principle follows from composing with a path tending to the point and uniqueness of limits; no packaged statement; the book states it in prose and shows it in Example 4.9. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.limit_fails_if_path_dependent |
| CA-514 | A point of S is an interior point if some disk about it lies in S, and a boundary point if every disk about it meets both S and its complement. | 3:§4.2 Definition (interior point, boundary point) p.330 | in Mathlib | interior, frontier, mem_interior_iff_mem_nhds, frontier_eq_closure_inter_closure | Mathlib: interior and frontier (topological, general form); Metric.mem_nhds_iff relates to disks. Book boundary point need not belong to S, matches frontier. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.interior_point_iff, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.boundary_point_iff |
| CA-515 | A set is open if every point is an interior point and closed if it contains all its boundary points. | 3:§4.2 Definition (open set, closed set) p.331 | in Mathlib | IsOpen, IsClosed, Metric.isOpen_iff, IsClosed.frontier_subset, frontier_subset_iff_isClosed, isOpen_iff_mem_nhds | General form (topological spaces); Metric.isOpen_iff is the ball formulation, frontier_subset_iff_isClosed the book's closedness criterion. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.open_set_iff, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.closed_set_iff |
| CA-516 | An open set is connected if it is not a union of two disjoint nonempty open sets; a region is an open, connected, nonempty set. | 3:§4.2 Definition (connected set, region) p.331 | in Mathlib | IsConnected, IsPreconnected, isPreconnected_iff_subset_of_disjoint, IsOpen | No named region; region = IsOpen s ∧ IsConnected s (IsPreconnected is the open-set condition; IsConnected adds nonempty). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.region_iff |
| CA-517 | A function of two (or more) variables is continuous at a point of its domain if the limit exists there, the function value exists, and they agree; equivalently by epsilon-delta. | 3:§4.2 Definition (continuity of a function of two variables) p.333 | in Mathlib | ContinuousAt, Metric.continuousAt_iff | General form (topological and metric spaces); Metric.continuousAt_iff is the epsilon-delta form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_iff_epsilon_delta |
| CA-518 | The sum of functions continuous at a point is continuous at that point. | 3:Theorem 4.2 p.333 | in Mathlib | ContinuousAt.add | General form (topological additive monoid). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_add |
| CA-519 | The product of functions continuous at a point is continuous at that point. | 3:Theorem 4.3 p.334 | in Mathlib | ContinuousAt.mul | General form (topological monoid). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_mul |
| CA-520 | If g is continuous at a point and h of one variable is continuous at g of that point, then h composed with g is continuous there. | 3:Theorem 4.4 p.334 | in Mathlib | ContinuousAt.comp | General form (topological spaces). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_comp |
| CA-521 | The partial derivatives of f(x,y) are the limits of the difference quotients in x with y fixed and in y with x fixed; they are computed by treating the other variable as constant and using the one-variable rules. | 3:§4.3 Definition (partial derivatives of a function of two variables) p.338 | in Mathlib | HasLineDerivAt, lineDeriv, HasDerivAt, hasDerivAt_iff_tendsto_slope | No dedicated name: partial derivative in x is HasDerivAt of t ↦ f(t,y) (or lineDeriv with direction (1,0)); general form (line derivative in any direction); the constant-variable computation rule is the definition. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.partial_derivatives_two_variables_iff |
| CA-522 | The partial derivatives of f(x,y,z) in x, y, z are the one-variable derivative limits holding the other two variables fixed. | 3:§4.3 Definition (partial derivatives of a function of three variables) p.343 | in Mathlib | HasLineDerivAt, lineDeriv | Line derivative along a coordinate direction (Pi.single i 1); general form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.partial_derivatives_three_variables_iff |
| CA-523 | Higher-order partial derivatives are partials of partials; mixed partials differentiate with respect to different variables. | 3:§4.3 Definition (higher-order and mixed partial derivatives) p.345 | not a result |  | Terminology (iterated partial derivatives); Mathlib uses iteratedFDeriv and fderiv of fderiv instead. |
| CA-524 | If the mixed second partial derivatives f_xy and f_yx are continuous on a disk around a point then they are equal there (Clairaut). | 3:Theorem 4.5 p.346 | in Mathlib | ContDiffAt.isSymmSndFDerivAt, second_derivative_symmetric_of_eventually, IsSymmSndFDerivAt | General form: second Fréchet derivative is a symmetric bilinear map for C^2 functions (hypothesis ContDiffAt with n ≥ 2), or for a function with first derivative nearby and a second derivative at the point; mixed partials are the values on basis vectors. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.clairaut |
| CA-525 | A partial differential equation is an equation involving an unknown function of several variables and some of its partial derivatives. | 3:§4.3 Definition (partial differential equation) p.347 | not a result |  | Terminology. |

| CA-527 | The tangent plane to a surface at a point is the plane containing the tangent lines of all curves through the point lying in the surface, when they are coplanar. | 3:§4.4 Definition (tangent plane) p.355 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.curve_tangent_in_tangent_plane | Mathlib has the derivative fderiv (graph approximated by an affine map) but no tangent plane of a surface. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.tangent_lines_lie_in_tangent_plane |
| CA-528 | For a differentiable f the tangent plane to z=f(x,y) at (a,b) is z = f(a,b) + f_x(a,b)(x-a) + f_y(a,b)(y-b). | 3:§4.4 Definition (equation of the tangent plane) p.356 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.tangentPlane, UndergradKB.Calculus.tangent_plane_unique | The affine function is the first-order approximation provided by HasFDerivAt; no statement about tangent planes. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.tangent_plane_equation |
| CA-529 | Near (a,b), f(x,y) is approximated by the tangent-plane function f(a,b)+f_x(a,b)(x-a)+f_y(a,b)(y-b). | 3:§4.4 Definition (linear approximation) p.359 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.linear_approximation, HasFDerivAt, hasFDerivAt_iff_isLittleO_nhds_zero | Mathlib's HasFDerivAt states f(x)-f(a)-f'(x-a)=o(x-a), which is the content (general form); no named linear approximation formula with partials. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.linear_approximation_two_variables |
| CA-530 | f is differentiable at (a,b) (resp. (a,b,c)) if f(x,y)-f(a,b) equals f_x(a,b)(x-a)+f_y(a,b)(y-b) plus an error term that is small compared to the distance to the point. | 3:§4.4 Definition (differentiable, two variables) p.361 · 3:§4.4 Definition (differentiable, three variables) p.366 | in Mathlib | DifferentiableAt, HasFDerivAt, hasFDerivAt_iff_isLittleO_nhds_zero | General form (Fréchet derivative on normed spaces); the book's coefficients are the partials of f. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiable_two_variables_iff, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiable_three_variables_iff |
| CA-531 | A function of two (or three) variables differentiable at a point is continuous at that point. | 3:Theorem 4.6 p.363 · 3:§4.4 Fact (differentiable implies continuous, three variables) p.366 | in Mathlib | DifferentiableAt.continuousAt, HasFDerivAt.continuousAt | General form (normed spaces). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_of_differentiableAt_two, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.continuousAt_of_differentiableAt_three |
| CA-532 | If the first partial derivatives of f exist near a point and are continuous at it, then f is differentiable there (also for three variables). | 3:Theorem 4.7 p.363 · 3:§4.4 Fact (continuous first partials imply differentiable, three variables) p.366 | in Mathlib | hasStrictFDerivAt_uncurry_coprod, HasStrictFDerivAt.hasFDerivAt | Bivariate curried form for f : E₁ → E₂ → F with partials near u continuous at u gives strict differentiability (stronger); n variables by nesting E₁ × E₂ with product spaces; general form (RCLike field). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.differentiableAt_of_continuous_partials; two variables only |
| CA-533 | The total differential is dz = f_x(a,b)dx + f_y(a,b)dy and approximates the change in f for small increments dx, dy. | 3:§4.4 Definition (differentials, total differential) p.364 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.totalDifferential, UndergradKB.Calculus.total_differential_approx, fderiv, HasFDerivAt, hasFDerivAt_iff_isLittleO_nhds_zero | The differential is fderiv applied to the increment and the approximation is the HasFDerivAt little-o statement; no separate notion of differentials. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.total_differential_approximates_increment |
| CA-534 | If x(t), y(t) are differentiable and z=f(x,y) is differentiable, then dz/dt = f_x dx/dt + f_y dy/dt. | 3:Theorem 4.8 p.369 | in Mathlib | HasFDerivAt.comp_hasDerivAt | General form: derivative of l ∘ f is l' applied to f' for f : 𝕜 → F and l differentiable on F; the book formula is obtained with F = ℝ², l' = (f_x, f_y). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.chain_rule_one_parameter |
| CA-535 | If x(u,v), y(u,v) are differentiable and z=f(x,y) is differentiable then z_u = f_x x_u + f_y y_u and z_v = f_x x_v + f_y y_v. | 3:Theorem 4.9 p.373 | in Mathlib | HasFDerivAt.comp | General form: Fréchet chain rule for compositions of maps between normed spaces; partial derivative formulas follow by evaluating at basis vectors. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.chain_rule_two_parameters |
| CA-536 | Generalized chain rule: for f of n variables each depending on m variables, the partial derivative with respect to a variable t_j is the sum over i of f_{x_i} times ∂x_i/∂t_j. | 3:Theorem 4.10 p.375 | in Mathlib | HasFDerivAt.comp | General form (Fréchet chain rule, any finite dimensions). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.generalized_chain_rule |
| CA-537 | If F(x,y)=0 defines y implicitly as a function of x then dy/dx = -F_x/F_y (F_y nonzero); if F(x,y,z)=0 defines z then z_x = -F_x/F_z and z_y = -F_y/F_z. | 3:Theorem 4.11 p.377 | in Mathlib | hasStrictFDerivAt_implicitFunctionOfBivariate, HasStrictFDerivAt.implicitFunctionOfProdDomain | General form: implicit function theorem for C^1-type bivariate maps (partials continuous, second one invertible) with derivative -(f₂)⁻¹ ∘ f₁; Mathlib constructs the implicit function rather than assuming it exists; hypotheses stronger than the book's. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.implicit_derivative_two_variables, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.implicit_derivative_three_variables_x, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.implicit_derivative_three_variables_y; book form (y given and differentiable), not the Mathlib implicit function construction |
| CA-538 | The directional derivative of f at a point in the direction of a unit vector u is the limit as t→0 of (f(point+tu)-f(point))/t; the same for three variables (unit vector components are the direction cosines). | 3:§4.6 Definition (directional derivative, two variables) p.383 · 3:§4.6 Definition (directional derivative, three variables) p.391 | in Mathlib | HasLineDerivAt, lineDeriv | General form (any vector, not just unit vectors; any normed space). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_iff |
| CA-539 | The gradient of f is the vector of its partial derivatives (f_x,f_y) (or (f_x,f_y,f_z)). | 3:§4.6 Definition (gradient, two variables) p.385 · 3:§4.6 Definition (gradient, three variables) p.390 | in Mathlib | gradient, HasGradientAt, inner_gradient_left, hasGradientAt_iff_hasFDerivAt | General form: gradient is the Riesz dual of fderiv on a real inner product space (use EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin n), since ℝ × ℝ has the sup norm); components equal partials by inner_gradient_left on basis vectors. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.gradient_component_eq_partial |
| CA-540 | For differentiable f the directional derivative in the unit direction u equals the gradient dotted with u (f_x cos θ + f_y sin θ in the plane). | 3:Theorem 4.12 p.384 · 3:Theorem 4.15 p.391 | in Mathlib | HasFDerivAt.hasLineDerivAt, inner_gradient_left | General form: HasFDerivAt f L x gives HasLineDerivAt f (L v) x v for every v; with the gradient, L v = ⟪∇f, v⟫ (inner_gradient_left). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_eq_inner_gradient, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_plane |
| CA-541 | If the gradient is zero all directional derivatives are zero; otherwise the directional derivative is largest (value \|∇f\|) in the gradient direction and smallest (value -\|∇f\|) in the opposite direction. | 3:Theorem 4.13 p.386 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_abs_le, UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_eq_zero_of_grad_zero, UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_max_iff, UndergradKB.Calculus.directionalDeriv_min_and_perp, real_inner_le_norm, inner_eq_norm_mul_iff_real, abs_real_inner_le_norm, HasFDerivAt.hasLineDerivAt | Cauchy-Schwarz pieces with equality case are present; the packaged statement about directional derivatives and the gradient is not. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.directional_derivative_extremes |

| CA-543 | A point is critical for f if both first partial derivatives vanish there or at least one does not exist. | 3:§4.7 Definition (critical point) p.395 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.IsCriticalPoint, UndergradKB.Calculus.isCriticalPoint_iff, UndergradKB.Calculus.isCriticalPoint_of_isLocalExtr | No named critical point for several variables; Mathlib states Fermat's theorem as fderiv = 0 (see v3c4-fermat-two-variables). / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.critical_point_characterization |
| CA-544 | f has a local maximum (minimum) at a point if f is at most (at least) its value there on a disk about the point; global when this holds on the whole domain. | 3:§4.7 Definition (local and global extrema) p.397 | in Mathlib | IsLocalMax, IsLocalMin, IsLocalExtr, IsMaxOn, IsMinOn | General form (topological space to ordered type, neighborhood instead of disk). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.local_max_iff_disk, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.local_min_iff_disk |

| CA-547 | With D = f_xx f_yy - (f_xy)^2 at a critical point: D>0 and f_xx>0 gives a local minimum, D>0 and f_xx<0 a local maximum, D<0 a saddle point, D=0 inconclusive. | 3:Theorem 4.17 p.398 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_min, UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_max, UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_saddle, UndergradKB.Calculus.second_derivative_test_inconclusive | Mathlib has only the one-variable second derivative test (isLocalMin_of_deriv_deriv_pos, Analysis/Calculus/DerivativeTest.lean); no Hessian test. / SeveralVariables.lean: f of class C² at the point; D = 0 inconclusive by x⁴+y⁴ and x⁴−y⁴. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.second_derivative_test |

| CA-549 | A continuous function on a closed bounded set in the plane attains an absolute maximum and an absolute minimum. | 3:Theorem 4.18 p.402 | in Mathlib | IsCompact.exists_isMinOn, IsCompact.exists_isMaxOn, Metric.isCompact_of_isClosed_isBounded | General form (compact sets in a topological space); closed and bounded implies compact in finite dimension via ProperSpace. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.extreme_value_closed_bounded |
| CA-550 | For a differentiable f on a closed bounded set, the absolute extreme values are among the values at interior critical points and on the boundary. | 3:Theorem 4.19 p.402 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.absolute_extrema_on_compact, IsCompact.exists_isMinOn, IsMinOn.isLocalMin, IsLocalExtr.fderiv_eq_zero | The ingredients (existence by compactness, interior global extremum is local, Fermat) are present; the packaged statement about candidates is not. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.absolute_extrema_closed_bounded |
| CA-551 | If f restricted to the curve g(x,y)=c has a local extremum at a point where the gradient of g is nonzero, then the gradient of f there equals lambda times the gradient of g; same method for three variables. | 3:Theorem 4.20 p.412 · 3:§4.8 Method (Lagrange multipliers, three variables) p.415 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier, UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier_two_vars, IsLocalExtrOn.exists_multipliers_of_hasStrictFDerivAt_1d, IsLocalExtrOn.exists_multipliers_of_hasStrictFDerivAt | Mathlib gives a nontrivial linear relation a f' + b φ' = 0 under strict differentiability (a, b) not both zero, general form in a normed space; the book's lambda form needs the extra step b ≠ 0 from the gradient of g being nonzero. / SeveralVariables.lean (from Mathlib's IsLocalExtrOn.exists_multipliers_of_hasStrictFDerivAt). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.lagrange_multipliers_one_constraint |
| CA-552 | With two constraints g=c and h=d, at a constrained local extremum the gradient of f is a combination lambda times gradient of g plus mu times gradient of h. | 3:§4.8 Method (Lagrange multipliers, two constraints) p.416 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier_two_constraints, UndergradKB.Calculus.lagrange_multiplier_two_constraints_two_vars, IsLocalExtrOn.exists_multipliers_of_hasStrictFDerivAt | Mathlib states linear dependence of the derivatives with a family of constraints (general form, finite index set); the book's form with independence of the constraint gradients is not stated. / SeveralVariables.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C4.lagrange_multipliers_two_constraints |
| | **16 Multiple integration** | | | | |
| CA-553 | The double integral of f over a rectangle R=[a,b]x[c,d] is the limit of the double Riemann sums sum f(x_ij*,y_ij*) dA as the grid is refined (m,n to infinity); for f>=0 it is the volume under the graph. | 3:Definition (double integral over a rectangle) p.430 · 3:Example 5.1 p.430 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_doubleSum, UndergradKB.Calculus.doubleSum_error_le, BoxIntegral.HasIntegral, MeasureTheory.integral | Mathlib's integral is the Lebesgue (Bochner) integral against volume on R x R; the Riemann-sum limit exists only as BoxIntegral (Riemann filter) on boxes, where it is not tied to the Lebesgue integral in one named statement here / MultipleRiemannSums.lean: for f continuous on the rectangle, double Riemann sums with any tags tend to the integral. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_rectangle_riemann_sums |
| CA-554 | A function that is bounded on R and continuous except on finitely many smooth curves (in particular a continuous function) is integrable over R. | 3:§5.1 text on integrability (bounded, continuous off finitely many smooth curves) p.432 | in Mathlib | BoxIntegral.integrable_of_continuousOn, BoxIntegral.integrable_of_bounded_and_ae_continuousWithinAt, ContinuousOn.integrableOn_compact | General form: bounded plus a.e. continuous suffices (null discontinuity set is weaker than finitely many curves); continuous on a compact box is integrable / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.integrable_of_continuousOn_rectangle; continuous case only; bounded with discontinuities on curves not stated |
| CA-555 | The double integral is linear: the integral of f+g is the sum of the integrals and the integral of cf is c times the integral of f. | 3:Theorem 5.1 (i),(ii) p.432 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.integral_add, MeasureTheory.integral_smul | General form for Bochner integrals on any measure space; the integrand must be integrable / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_add, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_const_mul |
| CA-556 | If a region is split into two pieces overlapping only in boundary (measure zero), the double integral over the union is the sum of the integrals over the pieces. | 3:Theorem 5.1 (iii) p.432 · 3:Theorem 5.5 p.454 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.setIntegral_union, MeasureTheory.setIntegral_union₀ | Theorem 5.5 (general regions) and Theorem 5.1(iii) (subregions of a rectangle) are the same result; Mathlib uses disjoint sets or a.e.-disjoint sets for overlap on a null boundary / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_union |
| CA-557 | If f<=g on a region then the double integral of f is at most the double integral of g. | 3:Theorem 5.1 (iv) p.432 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.setIntegral_mono_on | General form: set integrals on a measurable set, integrability of both functions assumed / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_mono |
| CA-558 | If m<=f<=M on R then m times the area of R is at most the double integral, which is at most M times the area. | 3:Theorem 5.1 (v) p.432 · 3:Example 5.4 p.435 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.setIntegral_mono_on, MeasureTheory.setIntegral_const, MeasureTheory.norm_setIntegral_le_of_norm_le_const | Follows from monotonicity and the integral of a constant; the book states it as its own item / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_bounds |
| CA-559 | If f(x,y)=g(x)h(y) then the double integral over [a,b]x[c,d] is the product of the two single integrals. | 3:Theorem 5.1 (vi) p.432 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod_mul, MeasureTheory.integral_prod_mul | General form for product measures on product sets of measurable sets / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_product |
| CA-560 | An iterated integral integrates first in one variable (other held fixed) and then in the other, in either order. | 3:Definition (iterated integral) p.433 | not a result | MeasureTheory.integral | Notation: a nested application of the integral; Mathlib writes it as integral over x of integral over y |
| CA-561 | Fubini: for f continuous (more generally bounded with discontinuities on finitely many curves, i.e. integrable) on a rectangle, the double integral equals either iterated integral. | 3:Theorem 5.2 p.433 · 3:Example 5.6 p.436 · 3:Example 5.7 p.437 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.integral_prod, MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod, MeasureTheory.integral_prod_symm, MeasureTheory.integral_integral_swap, ContinuousOn.integrableOn_compact | General form: any integrable function on a product of sigma-finite measure spaces; continuous on a compact rectangle gives integrability; swapping order of integration is integral_integral_swap / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_rectangle_dy_dx, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.fubini_rectangle_dx_dy; continuous integrand on a rectangle |
| CA-562 | The area of a plane region (rectangular or general bounded) is the double integral of the constant 1 over it. | 3:Definition (area of a rectangular region) p.439 · 3:Definition (area of a plane-bounded region) p.459 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.setIntegral_const | Mathlib: integral of the constant 1 over s equals the real measure of s (area is Lebesgue measure by definition); for regions between graphs see volume_regionBetween_eq_integral / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.area_eq_double_integral_one |
| CA-563 | For f>=0 the volume of the solid under the graph of f over a region is the double integral of f over the region. | 3:§5.1 text, volume V as double integral p.430 · 3:Example 5.9 p.439 | in Mathlib | volume_regionBetween_eq_integral | General form: measure of regionBetween f g s in alpha x R equals the integral of g-f over s; the region is an open-interval region in (R^2) x R, not literally R^3 / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.volume_under_graph |
| CA-564 | The average value of f over a region of positive area is the double integral of f divided by the area of the region. | 3:Definition (average value of f over a rectangle, eq. 5.4) p.440 · 3:Definition (average value over a plane region) p.459 · 3:Example 5.10 p.441 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.setAverage_eq | Definition of average over a set equals inverse of measure times integral; Mathlib's average is a definition and this lemma restates it / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.average_value_plane |
| CA-565 | A Type I region lies between two vertical lines and the graphs of two continuous functions of x; a Type II region lies between two horizontal lines and graphs of two continuous functions of y. | 3:Definition (Type I and Type II regions) p.449 · 3:Example 5.11 p.450 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.typeIRegion, UndergradKB.Calculus.typeIIRegion, UndergradKB.Calculus.isCompact_typeIRegion, regionBetween | Mathlib has regionBetween f g s (Type I shape, with open vertical interval) but no Type II definition and no continuity requirement / MultipleIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.type_I_and_II_regions_compact |
| CA-566 | The double integral over a bounded region D inside a rectangle R is defined as the integral over R of f extended by zero outside D; it exists when f is integrable on D. | 3:Theorem 5.3 p.451 · 3:§5.2 text on extending f by zero to a rectangle p.449 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.integral_indicator | In Mathlib the set integral is defined as the integral against the restricted measure and equals the integral of the indicator function (general form) / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.integral_over_region_via_zero_extension |
| CA-567 | For f continuous on a Type I region, the double integral equals the iterated integral with y from g1(x) to g2(x) inside and x from a to b outside; for a Type II region the order is reversed. | 3:Theorem 5.4 p.451 · 3:Example 5.12 p.451 · 3:Example 5.15 p.455 · 3:Example 5.16 p.456 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_typeI, UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_typeII, UndergradKB.Calculus.setIntegral_regionBetweenClosed, MeasureTheory.setIntegral_prod, MeasureTheory.integral_indicator, volume_regionBetween_eq_integral | Fubini on products of sets is there; the packaged iterated formula over a region between two graphs (variable inner limits) is not stated, only its measure version for regionBetween; follows by applying Fubini to the indicator function / MultipleIntegrals.lean: bounding functions continuous on ℝ. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.double_integral_type_I_and_II |
| CA-568 | Fubini for improper double integrals: for a nonnegative function with finitely many discontinuities on a bounded simple region, the improper double integral equals the iterated integral in either order. | 3:Theorem 5.6 p.461 · 3:Example 5.20 p.461 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.lintegral_prod, MeasureTheory.lintegral_prod_symm, MeasureTheory.setLIntegral_prod | General form: Tonelli for extended nonnegative functions on sigma-finite product measures; no notion of an improper Riemann integral / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.tonelli_nonneg; extended-real Tonelli on product regions |
| CA-569 | For f continuous on an unbounded rectangle such as [a,infty)x[c,infty), the improper double integral is the limit of the integrals over [a,b]x[c,d] as b,d tend to infinity when the limit exists. | 3:Theorem 5.7 p.462 · 3:Example 5.21 p.462 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_setIntegral_Icc_prod_Ici, UndergradKB.Calculus.tendsto_intervalIntegral_prod_Ici, MeasureTheory.tendsto_setIntegral_of_monotone | Mathlib gives convergence along any monotone exhaustion by measurable sets but only for an integrable function (absolute convergence); no statement specifically for rectangles or conditionally existing limits / MultipleIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.improper_double_integral_limit |
| CA-570 | A joint density f(x,y) of two continuous random variables gives P((X,Y) in D) as the double integral of f over D, with f>=0 and total integral 1. | 3:Definition (joint density function) p.463 · 3:Example 5.22 p.463 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.IsJointDensity.measure_preimage, UndergradKB.Calculus.IsJointDensity.lintegral_eq_one, MeasureTheory.pdf.integral_pdf_smul | Mathlib has HasPDF/pdf for random variables valued in any space (including R x R) and the expectation formula; no named statement P((X,Y) in D) = integral of density over D for the pair / MultipleIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.joint_density_function |

| CA-593 | The radii of gyration are the square roots of the moments of inertia divided by the mass (about the x-axis, y-axis, and origin). | 3:§5.6 radii of gyration p.543 · 3:Example 5.61 p.544 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationX_sq, UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationY_sq, UndergradKB.Calculus.MassMoments.laminaRadiusGyrationO_sq | Not in Mathlib / MassMoments.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.lamina_radii_of_gyration |

| CA-598 | A transformation (change of variables) T(u,v)=(x(u,v),y(u,v)) maps a region in the uv-plane to a region in the xy-plane; it is C^1 if the component functions have continuous first partial derivatives. | 3:§5.7 text, planar transformation p.554 · 3:§5.7 text, C^1 transformation p.556 · 3:Example 5.66 p.555 | not a result |  | Terminology; in Mathlib a C^1 map is ContDiff 1 or one with HasFDerivWithinAt |
| CA-599 | A transformation is one-to-one if no two distinct points map to the same image point. | 3:Definition (one-to-one transformation) p.554 · 3:Example 5.65 p.554 | in Mathlib | Set.InjOn | Injectivity on the region; book's inverse remark is Function.invFunOn / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.one_to_one_transformation |
| CA-600 | The Jacobian of T(u,v)=(x,y) is the 2x2 determinant of the partial derivatives, x_u y_v - x_v y_u. | 3:Definition (Jacobian of a C^1 transformation, two variables) p.557 · 3:Example 5.68 p.558 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.integral_image_eq_integral_abs_det_fderiv_smul | Mathlib uses the determinant of the Frechet derivative (f' x).det, which is the Jacobian determinant in any dimension; no separate definition named Jacobian for the 2x2 case / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.jacobian_two_variables |
| CA-601 | The Jacobian of the polar coordinate map (r,theta) to (r cos t, r sin t) is r, and the cylindrical coordinate map has Jacobian r as well. | 3:Example 5.67 p.557 · 3:Example 5.72 (a) p.564 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.det_polar_jacobian, UndergradKB.Calculus.det_cylindrical_jacobian, det_fderivPolarCoordSymm | Polar case: determinant of the derivative of polarCoord.symm equals r; the cylindrical case (with z) is not stated / MultipleIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.jacobian_polar_and_cylindrical |
| CA-602 | Change of variables for double integrals: for a one-to-one C^1 map T with nonzero Jacobian taking S onto R, the integral of f over R equals the integral over S of f(T(u,v)) \|Jacobian\| du dv. | 3:Theorem 5.14 p.559 · 3:Example 5.69 p.559 · 3:Example 5.70 p.560 · 3:Example 5.71 p.562 · 3:§5.7 Problem-Solving Strategy p.561 | in Mathlib | MeasureTheory.integral_image_eq_integral_abs_det_fderiv_smul, MeasureTheory.lintegral_image_eq_lintegral_abs_det_fderiv_mul | General form: injective on a measurable set s with derivative within s (no nonzero-Jacobian hypothesis needed); in any finite-dimensional real space / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C5.change_of_variables_double |

| CA-605 | A vector field on a subset D of the plane or space assigns a 2- or 3-component vector to each point of D; it is continuous when its component functions are. | 3:§6.1 Definition (vector field) p.585 · 3:Equation 6.1 and 6.2 p.586 | not a result |  | Terminology: just a function D -> R^n; Mathlib has no separate notion. |

| CA-607 | A vector field F is a gradient (conservative) field if F = grad f for some scalar function f; f is called a potential function of F. | 3:§6.1 Definition (gradient field) p.595 · 3:§6.1 Definition (conservative field, potential function) p.596 · 3:Key Concepts 6.1 p.747 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservativeOn, UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative2, UndergradKB.Calculus.IsConservative3, gradient, HasGradientAt | Gradient and HasGradientAt exist (any real inner product space); there is no named predicate for conservative fields, it is just exists f, forall x, HasGradientAt f (F x) x. / LineIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gradient_field_definition |
| CA-608 | On an open connected domain, two potential functions of the same conservative field differ by a constant. | 3:Theorem 6.1 p.597 | in Mathlib | IsOpen.exists_eq_add_of_fderiv_eq | General form: open + IsPreconnected set, f and g differentiable on it with equal fderiv give a constant a with f = g + a on the set; gradient equality is the same as fderiv equality (Riesz). Also IsOpen.exists_is_const_of_fderiv_eq_zero. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.potential_unique_up_to_constant |
| CA-609 | If F=(P,Q) (or (P,Q,R)) is conservative with C^1 components then the mixed partials agree: P_y=Q_x (and P_z=R_x, Q_z=R_y). | 3:Theorem 6.2 p.598 | in Mathlib | second_derivative_symmetric, ContDiffAt.isSymmSndFDerivAt | General form: symmetry of the second Frechet derivative of the potential f (f'' v w = f'' w v for f' = fderiv f), which is Clairaut applied to f; book statement follows by taking v,w to be coordinate vectors. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.mixed_partials_of_potential, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.mixed_partials_plane |
| CA-610 | The scalar line integral of f along a smooth curve C is the limit of Riemann sums sum f(P_i*) Delta s_i over a partition of C into arc-length pieces. | 3:§6.2 Definition (scalar line integral) p.602 · 3:Equation 6.5 p.602 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.LineIntegralLimits.line_integral_riemann_sums, UndergradKB.Calculus.LineIntegralLimits.line_integral_riemann_sums_plane | Mathlib has no line integral with respect to arc length (only curveIntegral of 1-forms). / LineIntegralLimits.lean: chord-length sums on the regular partition tend to the integral. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.scalar_line_integral_riemann_sums |

Riemann-sum definition, arbitrary parameter interval and invariance under reparameterization not there.v3c6-line-integral-differential-notation / LineIntegrals.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.vector_line_integral_invariance |

Ingredients: curve integral as integral of omega(gamma t)(gamma' t), the one-variable FTC and the chain rule (HasFDerivAt composition in Mathlib.Analysis.Calculus.FDeriv.Comp); the exact statement is not in Mathlib. / LineIntegrals.lean: C¹ curves (piecewise smooth curves by additivity are not stated). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.fundamental_theorem_line_integrals |

| CA-629 | A continuous vector field that is independent of path on an open connected domain is conservative (potential defined by integrating from a base point). | 3:Theorem 6.9 p.631 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.exists_potential_of_triangle | Mathlib only has the primitive-by-segment-integral construction for closed forms on convex sets (Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric), not this statement. / LineIntegrals.lean: on open convex sets (zero circulation around triangles gives a potential); the open connected case needs integrals along piecewise C¹ (polygonal) paths, not in Mathlib. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.path_independent_field_conservative |
| CA-630 | On an open simply connected region, a C^1 field F=(P,Q) with P_y=Q_x (resp. all three cross partials equal in space) is conservative. | 3:Theorem 6.10 p.636 · 3:Example 6.34 p.637 · 3:Example 6.35 p.637 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_of_pdy_eq_pdx, UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative3_iff_curl3_eq_zero, Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric, Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivWithinAt_of_fderivWithin_symmetric | Poincare lemma for 1-forms: on an OPEN CONVEX set, a differentiable omega with symmetric fderiv has a primitive f with HasFDerivAt f (omega a) a. General form (any normed space) but convex only; the simply connected case is not there (file comments say it is planned via homotopy invariance of curve integrals). / DivCurl.lean: on open convex sets (the book: simply connected); from Mathlib's Poincaré lemma. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.cross_partials_imply_conservative |
| CA-631 | On an open simply connected region, F is conservative if and only if its cross partials are equal (P_y=Q_x in the plane; in space also P_z=R_x and Q_z=R_y). | 3:Theorem 6.11 p.637 · 3:Key Concepts 6.3 p.748 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.isConservative2_iff_pdy_eq_pdx, UndergradKB.Calculus.pdy2_eq_pdx2_of_isConservative2, second_derivative_symmetric, Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric | Forward direction in Mathlib in full (symmetry of second derivative); converse only for open convex sets. / DivCurl.lean: on open convex sets (the book: simply connected). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.conservative_iff_cross_partials_equal |
| CA-632 | Green's theorem (circulation form): for a positively oriented piecewise smooth simple closed curve C bounding D, the circulation of F=(P,Q) equals the double integral over D of Q_x - P_y. | 3:Theorem 6.12 p.644 · 3:Equation 6.13 p.644 · 3:Example 6.38 p.646 · 3:Example 6.39 p.647 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cell, UndergradKB.Calculus.OrientedCurve.IsOpposite, UndergradKB.Calculus.sum_map_cancel_pairs, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_param_image, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_param, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_pullback_rect, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_circulation_rect, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_typeI_P, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_typeII_Q, MeasureTheory.integral2_divergence_prod_of_hasFDerivAt, MeasureTheory.integral2_divergence_prod_of_hasFDerivAt_off_countable | Only for rectangles: iterated integral over [a1,b1]x[a2,b2] of f_x + g_y equals boundary integrals of f and g along the four sides (continuous on closed rectangle, HasFDerivAt in the interior, divergence integrable). Take f=Q, g=-P. No general regions bounded by simple closed curves and no curve-integral form. / , GreenParam.lean / GreenRegions.lean: Green's theorem (circulation form) for every region D = Φ(S) with Φ a C² map of a rectangle, injective and orientation preserving on S (S between the open and the closed rectangle): disks and ellipses (polar coordinates), triangles, type I and type II regions; the boundary is the image of the rectangle's boundary. Proof: Mathlib's rectangle theorem pulled back along Φ (second derivatives cancel by symmetry) and the change of variables formula. , Chains.lean / GreenGlued.lean: for every region glued from finitely many parametrized cells (each a C² image of a rectangle as above, pairwise overlapping in measure zero, interior edges traversed once in each direction up to a C¹ change of parameter), proved by cancelling the interior edges. Circulation form: ∬_D (Q_x - P_y) dA equals the sum of the line integrals along the outer edges. Still not covered: a region bounded by an arbitrary piecewise smooth simple closed curve; missing lemma: the Jordan curve theorem and the existence of a decomposition (triangulation) of every Jordan region with piecewise smooth boundary into parametrized cells; neither is in Mathlib. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_glued_region, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_circulation_param_region |
| CA-633 | Area of a region D bounded by a positively oriented simple closed curve C is the integral over C of x dy, equivalently of -y dx, equivalently one half of the integral of x dy - y dx (e.g. area of an ellipse x^2/a^2+y^2/b^2=1 is pi a b). | 3:Example 6.40 p.648 · 3:Equation 6.14 p.650 · 3:Example 6.41 p.651 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.area_glued, UndergradKB.Calculus.area_param_image, UndergradKB.Calculus.area_param_image_of_curl2_eq_one, UndergradKB.Calculus.rect_area_line_integrals, UndergradKB.Calculus.area_typeI_eq_neg_line_integral, UndergradKB.Calculus.area_typeII_eq_line_integral | Consequence of Green's theorem; not in Mathlib (the rectangle Green's theorem does not cover curves). / , GreenRegions.lean: area of D = Φ(S) (parametrized region as in CA-632) as ∮ x dy, -∮ y dx and ½∮ x dy - y dx along the image of the rectangle's boundary. , Chains.lean / GreenGlued.lean: for every region glued from finitely many parametrized cells (each a C² image of a rectangle as above, pairwise overlapping in measure zero, interior edges traversed once in each direction up to a C¹ change of parameter), proved by cancelling the interior edges. Area of D as ∮ x dy, -∮ y dx and ½∮ (x dy - y dx) along the outer edges. Still not covered: a region bounded by an arbitrary piecewise smooth simple closed curve; missing lemma: the Jordan curve theorem and the existence of a decomposition (triangulation) of every Jordan region with piecewise smooth boundary into parametrized cells; neither is in Mathlib. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_glued_region_line_integrals, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.area_param_region_line_integrals |
| CA-634 | Green's theorem (flux form): the flux of F=(P,Q) across the positively oriented boundary C of D equals the double integral over D of P_x + Q_y. | 3:Theorem 6.13 p.650 · 3:Equation 6.15 p.650 · 3:Example 6.42 p.652 · 3:Example 6.43 p.653 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_glued, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_param_image, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_param, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_flux_rect, MeasureTheory.integral2_divergence_prod_of_hasFDerivAt, MeasureTheory.integral2_divergence_prod_of_hasFDerivAt_off_countable | Same rectangle-only theorem with f=P, g=Q (this is exactly the divergence form of Mathlib's statement). General regions missing. / , GreenRegions.lean: flux form ∬_D (P_x + Q_y) dA = ∮ P dy - Q dx for parametrized regions D = Φ(S) as in CA-632. , Chains.lean / GreenGlued.lean: for every region glued from finitely many parametrized cells (each a C² image of a rectangle as above, pairwise overlapping in measure zero, interior edges traversed once in each direction up to a C¹ change of parameter), proved by cancelling the interior edges. Flux form: ∬_D (P_x + Q_y) dA = ∮ P dy - Q dx along the outer edges. Still not covered: a region bounded by an arbitrary piecewise smooth simple closed curve; missing lemma: the Jordan curve theorem and the existence of a decomposition (triangulation) of every Jordan region with piecewise smooth boundary into parametrized cells; neither is in Mathlib. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_glued_region, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_flux_param_region |
| CA-635 | A field is source free if its flux across every closed curve is zero; a stream function of F=(P,Q) is g with g_y=P, g_x=-Q. | 3:§6.4 text (source free) p.653 · 3:§6.4 Definition (stream function) p.654 · 3:Example 6.44 p.654 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction, UndergradKB.Calculus.IsSourceFree2, UndergradKB.Calculus.IsStreamFunction.isSourceFree2 | Terminology; not in Mathlib. / DivCurl.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_and_stream_function |
| CA-636 | On a simply connected domain these are equivalent for a field F: flux across any closed curve is zero; flux is path independent; F has a stream function; P_x+Q_y=0. | 3:§6.4 List (equivalent conditions for source free) p.653 · 3:Key Concepts 6.4 p.748 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.sourceFree_tfae, UndergradKB.Calculus.IsFluxPathIndependent2 | Analogue of the conservative field equivalences; no proof in the book. / SourceFree.lean: the four conditions (zero flux across closed curves, path independence, stream function, div F = 0) are equivalent on open convex domains (book: simply connected). Missing in Mathlib for the simply connected case: the Poincaré lemma for closed 1-forms on simply connected open sets (Mathlib has it for convex sets, Convex.exists_forall_hasFDerivAt_of_fderiv_symmetric, and invariance under C² homotopies, ContinuousMap.Homotopy.curveIntegral_add_curveIntegral_eq_of_hasFDerivWithinAt, but not the smoothing of continuous homotopies). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.source_free_equivalent_conditions |
| CA-637 | A function satisfying Laplace's equation (sum of second partials equals 0) is called harmonic. | 3:§6.4 Definition (harmonic function) p.655 | in Mathlib | InnerProductSpace.HarmonicAt, InnerProductSpace.HarmonicOnNhd | Mathlib definition: C^2 near x and Laplacian zero near x, on any finite-dimensional real inner product space (general form). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.harmonic_iff_laplace |
| CA-638 | A potential function of a conservative source-free field (in the plane, simply connected domain) is harmonic. | 3:§6.4 Fact (potentials of source-free conservative fields are harmonic) p.655 · 3:Example 6.45 p.655 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.laplacian2, UndergradKB.Calculus.laplacian2_eq_zero_of_potential | Short consequence of equality of mixed partials and div F = 0. / DivCurl.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.potential_of_source_free_field_harmonic |
| CA-639 | Green's theorem extends to regions with finitely many holes: the double integral over D equals the line integral over the whole boundary, outer curve counterclockwise and inner curves clockwise (both forms). | 3:§6.4 Theorem (Green's theorem on general regions) p.656 · 3:Key Equations 6 p.747 · 3:Example 6.46 p.657 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.green_glued, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_cells, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_between_curves, UndergradKB.Calculus.green_param_image | Not in Mathlib. (Related, not equal: Cauchy-Goursat for rectangles and circle integrals in Mathlib.Analysis.Complex.CauchyIntegral.) / , GreenHoles.lean: Green's theorem for a region with one hole swept by the segments from an inner closed C² curve γ₀ to an outer one γ₁ (Φ(s,t) = (1-s)γ₀(t) + sγ₁(t) injective and orientation preserving): ∬_D (Q_x - P_y) = ∮_{γ₁} - ∮_{γ₀} (inner boundary clockwise). , Chains.lean / GreenGlued.lean: for every region glued from finitely many parametrized cells (each a C² image of a rectangle as above, pairwise overlapping in measure zero, interior edges traversed once in each direction up to a C¹ change of parameter), proved by cancelling the interior edges. The outer edges may form any number of closed curves, so regions with finitely many holes are covered (inner boundaries clockwise). Still not covered: a region bounded by an arbitrary piecewise smooth simple closed curve; missing lemma: the Jordan curve theorem and the existence of a decomposition (triangulation) of every Jordan region with piecewise smooth boundary into parametrized cells; neither is in Mathlib. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_glued_region_with_holes, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.green_theorem_region_between_curves |
| CA-640 | For F=(-y,x)/(x^2+y^2) and any positively oriented simple closed curve C missing the origin, the circulation is 0 if C does not enclose the origin and 2 pi if it does. | 3:Example 6.47 p.658 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_mem_zmultiples, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_glued_annulus, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_glued_zero, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_avoiding_ray, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_polar_graph, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_homotopic_circle, UndergradKB.Calculus.curl2_swirl, UndergradKB.Calculus.circulation_homotopy, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_origin_circle, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_closed_right, UndergradKB.Calculus.swirl_circulation_circle_not_enclosing | Needs the extended Green theorem; not in Mathlib for general C. / , GreenHoles.lean: the swirl field is curl free off the origin; its circulation is 0 around every closed C² curve missing a ray from the origin, 2π around every closed polar graph r = ρ(θ) > 0 (star shaped about the origin) and around every closed curve C²-homotopic to the unit circle in the punctured plane (homotopy invariance of the circulation of curl-free fields). , WindingNumber.lean: the circulation along every closed C¹ curve avoiding the origin is 2π times an integer (the winding number; proof by the complex logarithmic derivative z'/z); it is 2π along every curve that, with a small circle about the origin traversed clockwise, bounds a region glued from parametrized cells avoiding the origin, and 0 along the boundary of every such region avoiding the origin. Still not covered: an arbitrary simple closed curve; missing lemma: the Jordan curve theorem (a Jordan curve has winding number 0 or ±1 about a point off it, according as the point is outside or inside), not in Mathlib. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_winding_number, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_glued_enclosing, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_glued_not_enclosing, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.swirl_circulation_cases |
| CA-641 | The divergence of a field F=(P,Q,R) is div F = P_x + Q_y + R_z (planar: P_x+Q_y), a scalar function. | 3:§6.5 Definition (divergence) p.668 · 3:Equation 6.16 p.668 · 3:Example 6.48 p.670 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.div2, UndergradKB.Calculus.div3 | No named divergence for vector fields in Mathlib; the expression sum f' x (e i) i only appears inside the divergence theorem statements. / DivCurl.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_formula |

DivCurl.lean: the stream-function form. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stream_function_field_divergence_free |

| CA-649 | Standard parameterizations: cylinder x^2+y^2=r^2 by (r cos u, r sin u, v); cone z=k sqrt(x^2+y^2) by (u cos v, u sin v, ku); sphere by spherical coordinates (r sin phi cos theta, r sin phi sin theta, r cos phi); graph z=f(x,y) by (x,y,f(x,y)); surface of revolution (x, f(x) cos t, f(x) sin t). | 3:Example 6.58 p.682 · 3:Example 6.60 p.685 · 3:§6.6 Fact (sphere and graph parameterizations) p.685 · 3:Example 6.64 p.693 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.sphereParam, UndergradKB.Calculus.cylinderParam, UndergradKB.Calculus.coneParam, UndergradKB.Calculus.graphParam, UndergradKB.Calculus.revolutionParam | Formulas; spherical coordinates for the integral exist in Mathlib but not as surface parameterizations. / Surfaces.lean (definitions). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.standard_surface_parameterizations |

| CA-658 | Stokes' theorem: for a piecewise smooth oriented surface S whose boundary is a positively oriented simple closed curve C and a C^1 field F, the circulation of F along C equals the flux of curl F across S. | 3:Theorem 6.19 p.714 · 3:Example 6.73 p.717 · 3:Example 6.75 p.720 · 3:Key Equations 6 p.747 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_glued, UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_patch, UndergradKB.Calculus.ParamRect.boundary, UndergradKB.Calculus.spaceCurveIntegral, UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_param, UndergradKB.Calculus.curlVec, UndergradKB.Calculus.stokes_flat_rect | Not in Mathlib (no curl, surface integrals); Green's circulation form is the planar special case. / , StokesParam.lean: Stokes' theorem for every surface with a C² parametrization r by a rectangle (flux of curl F across the surface oriented by r_u × r_v equals the circulation along the image of the rectangle's boundary; graphs, hemispheres, cylinders, cones). , Chains.lean / StokesGlued.lean: Stokes' theorem for every surface glued from finitely many C² patches with compatible orientations (each interior edge traversed once in each direction up to a C¹ change of parameter): the flux of curl F (sum of the patch fluxes) equals the circulation along the outer edges. This is the book's piecewise smooth surface given as a union of parametrized pieces; the only remaining restriction is that the pieces are images of rectangles (triangular pieces are degenerate rectangles). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_glued_surface, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.stokes_theorem_param_surface |

The only remaining restriction is that the pieces are images of rectangles. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_flux_glued_surfaces_equal, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.curl_flux_same_boundary_equal |

| CA-661 | Divergence theorem: for a closed piecewise smooth outward-oriented surface S enclosing a solid E and a C^1 field F, the flux of F across S equals the triple integral of div F over E. | 3:Theorem 6.20 p.730 · 3:Equation 6.24 p.730 · 3:Example 6.77 p.731 · 3:Example 6.78 p.734 · 3:Key Equations 6 p.747 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued, UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_cell, UndergradKB.Calculus.face_integral_eq_patchFlux, UndergradKB.Calculus.SolidCell.faces, UndergradKB.Calculus.patchFlux_of_isOpposite, UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_param_image, UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_param_box, UndergradKB.Calculus.divVec, UndergradKB.Calculus.jacobian3, UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_theorem_box, MeasureTheory.integral_divergence_of_hasFDerivAt_off_countable, MeasureTheory.integral_divergence_of_hasFDerivAt_off_countable', MeasureTheory.integral_divergence_of_hasFDerivAt_off_countable_of_equiv | Mathlib proves it only for boxes (Icc a b in R^(n+1)): integral of sum_i f'_i(x)(e_i) over the box equals sum over faces of the front minus back face integrals, with f continuous on the box, differentiable off a countable set in the interior, divergence integrable. No general solids/surfaces (no surface integral exists). / , DivergenceParam.lean: divergence theorem for every solid E = Φ(S) with Φ a C² map of a box, injective and orientation preserving on S (balls, cylinders, solids between graphs): ∭_E div F dV equals the outward flux through the six parametrized faces; parameter form divergence_param_box without injectivity. , Chains.lean / DivergenceGlued.lean: for every solid glued from finitely many parametrized cells (C² images of boxes, overlapping in measure zero) whose faces are, up to order, the outer boundary patches and pairs of opposite patches (interior walls), ∭_E div F dV equals the outward flux through the outer patches. Still not covered: a solid bounded by an arbitrary closed piecewise smooth surface; missing lemma: the Jordan-Brouwer separation theorem and the decomposition of the enclosed solid into parametrized cells (not in Mathlib). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_glued_solid, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.divergence_theorem_param_solid |

| CA-664 | The flux of r/\|r\|^3 across a closed piecewise smooth surface S is 0 if S does not enclose the origin and 4 pi if it does. | 3:Theorem 6.21 p.738 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_glued_shell, UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_glued_zero, UndergradKB.Calculus.divergence_glued_invSquare, UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_radialGraph, UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_param_solid_eq_zero, UndergradKB.Calculus.divVec_invSquareField, UndergradKB.Calculus.flux_invSquare_sphere | Needs the extended divergence theorem for solids with holes; not in Mathlib. / , InverseSquareFlux.lean / DivergenceInvSquare.lean: flux 4π through every closed surface star shaped about the origin (radial graph r = ρ(φ,θ)u(φ,θ), ρ > 0); flux 0 out of every C²-parametrized solid not containing the origin (div (r/‖r‖³) = 0 off the origin). , DivergenceGlued.lean: the extended divergence theorem for a solid with a spherical hole: flux 4π through every closed surface S that, with a sphere about the origin oriented inward, bounds a solid glued from parametrized cells avoiding the origin; flux 0 through the boundary of every glued solid avoiding the origin. | CA-665 | Gauss' law: the flux of the electrostatic field across a closed surface is the enclosed charge divided by the electric constant. | 3:§6.8 Gauss' law p.739 · 3:Example 6.81 p.739 · 3:Key Concepts 6.8 p.749 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_shell, UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_glued_no_charge, UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_radialGraph, UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_param_solid_no_charge, UndergradKB.Calculus.gauss_law_sphere | Physical law derived from Theorem 6.21 (point charge); not a Mathlib statement. / , InverseSquareFlux.lean / DivergenceInvSquare.lean: Gauss' law q/ε₀ for every closed surface star shaped about the charge, and 0 for the boundary of every C²-parametrized solid not containing the charge. , DivergenceGlued.lean: Gauss' law q/ε₀ through every closed surface that, with a sphere about the charge, bounds a glued solid avoiding the charge, and 0 for the boundary of a glued solid avoiding the charge. Still not covered: a solid bounded by an arbitrary closed piecewise smooth surface; missing lemma: the Jordan-Brouwer separation theorem and the decomposition of the enclosed solid into parametrized cells (not in Mathlib). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_glued_solid, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C6.gauss_law_enclosed_or_not |
| | **18 Second-order differential equations** | | | | |
| CA-666 | A second-order equation is linear when it has the form y'' + p(x)y' + q(x)y = f(x); it is homogeneous when f is identically zero and nonhomogeneous otherwise. | 3:§7.1 Definition (linear, homogeneous, nonhomogeneous second-order equation) p.752 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.LinSol, UndergradKB.Calculus.ConstSol | Terminology only; Mathlib has no second-order linear ODE API (Mathlib/Analysis/ODE has only first-order Picard-Lindelof, Gronwall). / SecondOrderODE.lean: solution predicates (solutions on all of ℝ). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.linear_second_order_equation_definition |
| CA-667 | Any linear combination c1 y1 + c2 y2 of two solutions of a linear homogeneous equation is again a solution. | 3:Theorem 7.1 p.754 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.superposition | Follows from linearity of deriv/iteratedDeriv (deriv_add, deriv_const_smul) but no ODE-level statement exists. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.superposition_principle |
| CA-668 | Functions f1..fn are linearly dependent on an interval if some nontrivial combination vanishes identically there; otherwise they are linearly independent. | 3:§7.1 Definition (linear dependence of functions) p.755 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.LinDepOn, LinearIndependent | General form for vectors in a module (functions as vectors); the interval restriction is not built in (take functions restricted to the interval as a subtype). / SecondOrderODE.lean (two functions). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.linear_dependence_of_functions |
| CA-669 | Two functions are linearly dependent exactly when one is identically zero or one is a constant multiple of the other. | 3:Theorem 7.2 p.755 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.linDepOn_iff, LinearIndependent.pair_iff' | General form for vectors in a vector space, hypothesis x nonzero (book also handles the zero case, trivially); function-on-interval instance not stated. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.linearly_dependent_iff_constant_multiple |
| CA-670 | If y1, y2 are linearly independent solutions of a second-order linear homogeneous equation, then c1 y1 + c2 y2 is the general solution (every solution has this form). | 3:Theorem 7.3 p.756 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_of_independent | Book omits the proof; Mathlib has no solution-space dimension result for linear ODEs. / SecondOrderODE.lean: continuous p, q; via uniqueness for the initial value problem (Gronwall-type energy argument). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.general_solution_linearly_independent |
| CA-671 | For a y'' + b y' + c y = 0 with constant coefficients, the characteristic equation is a r^2 + b r + c = 0 (from trying y = e^(r x)). | 3:§7.1 Definition (characteristic equation) p.757 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.exp_solves_iff_characteristic | Definition; the substitution uses derivative of exp (HasDerivAt.exp) only. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.characteristic_equation |
| CA-672 | If the characteristic equation has distinct real roots r1, r2, the general solution is c1 e^(r1 x) + c2 e^(r2 x). | 3:§7.1 Distinct Real Roots (unnumbered) p.757 · 3:Table 7.1 p.759 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_distinct_real_roots | Verification that e^(rx) solves uses HasDerivAt.exp, but no ODE statement. / SecondOrderODE.lean: both directions (every solution has this form). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_distinct_real_roots |
| CA-673 | If the characteristic equation has a repeated real root r, the general solution is c1 e^(r x) + c2 x e^(r x). | 3:§7.1 Single Repeated Real Root (unnumbered) p.757 · 3:Table 7.1 p.759 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_repeated_root | SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_repeated_real_root |
| CA-674 | If the characteristic roots are complex conjugates a plus or minus b i, the general real solution is e^(a x)(c1 cos b x + c2 sin b x). | 3:§7.1 Complex Conjugate Roots (unnumbered) p.758 · 3:Table 7.1 p.759 · 3:Example 7.6 p.759 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.general_solution_complex_roots | Derivation uses Euler's formula (Complex.exp_mul_I) but the ODE statement is absent. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.solution_complex_conjugate_roots |
| CA-675 | A boundary-value problem is a differential equation together with conditions on the solution at two different values of the variable. | 3:§7.1 boundary conditions / boundary-value problem (unnumbered) p.760 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.IsBVPSolution, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.bvp_sin_infinitely_many, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.bvp_sin_unique | Terminology; compare initial-value problems (Picard-Lindelof in Mathlib handles first-order case). / ODEExtras.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.boundary_value_problem_examples |
| CA-676 | For a nonhomogeneous linear equation, the associated homogeneous equation with right-hand side zero is called the complementary equation. | 3:§7.2 Definition (complementary equation, Eq. 7.3) p.764 | not a result |  | Terminology. |
| CA-677 | A particular solution is a solution containing no arbitrary constants. | 3:§7.2 Definition (particular solution) p.765 | not a result |  | Terminology. |
| CA-678 | The general solution of y'' + p y' + q y = f is a particular solution plus the general solution of the complementary equation. | 3:Theorem 7.4 p.765 · 3:Example 7.11 p.765 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.general_eq_particular_add_complementary | Proof in book is short (difference of two solutions solves the homogeneous equation). / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.general_solution_particular_plus_complementary |
| CA-679 | Method of undetermined coefficients: when f is built from polynomials, exponentials, sines and cosines, guess a particular solution of the same form (table of guesses), multiplying by x as needed to avoid overlap with complementary solutions, then solve for the coefficients. | 3:Table 7.2 p.767 · 3:§7.2 Strategy: Undetermined Coefficients p.768 · 3:Example 7.12 p.766 · 3:Example 7.13 p.767 · 3:Example 7.14 p.769 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_exp, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_exp_resonant, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_trig, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.undetermined_polynomial | Technique; no theorem justifying the guess forms is stated in the book either. / ODEExtras.lean: exponential (also at a simple root), trigonometric and polynomial right-hand sides. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.undetermined_coefficients_guesses |
| CA-680 | Cramer's rule: a 2 by 2 linear system with nonzero determinant has solutions given by ratios of determinants. | 3:§7.2 Rule: Cramer's Rule p.771 · 3:Example 7.15 p.771 | in Mathlib | Matrix.cramer, Matrix.cramer_apply, Matrix.mulVec_cramer | General form for n by n matrices over a commutative ring: A *ᵥ cramer A b = det A • b; the solution formula follows by dividing by det A (see also Matrix.cramer_eq_adjugate_mulVec). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.cramers_rule_two_by_two, UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.cramers_rule_matrix |
| CA-681 | Variation of parameters: with independent complementary solutions y1, y2, a particular solution is u1 y1 + u2 y2 where u1' y1 + u2' y2 = 0 and u1' y1' + u2' y2' = f, solved by Cramer's rule and integration. | 3:§7.2 Strategy: Variation of Parameters p.772 · 3:Example 7.16 p.773 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.variation_of_parameters, UndergradKB.Calculus.variation_of_parameters_cramer | Technique with derivation in text; no Mathlib ODE support. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.variation_of_parameters_method |
| CA-682 | An undamped spring-mass system m x'' + k x = 0 gives simple harmonic motion x = c1 cos(w t) + c2 sin(w t) with w = sqrt(k/m). | 3:§7.3 Equations 7.5-7.6 (simple harmonic motion) p.776 · 3:Example 7.17 p.777 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.simple_harmonic_motion | Special case of the constant-coefficient complex-roots solution; Mathlib has derivatives of sin and cos but not this ODE. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.simple_harmonic_motion_solutions |
| CA-683 | c1 cos(w t) + c2 sin(w t) can be rewritten as A sin(w t + phi), with A = sqrt(c1^2 + c2^2) and phi determined by tan phi = c1/c2 in the correct quadrant. | 3:Theorem 7.5 p.778 · 3:Example 7.18 p.779 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.amplitude_phase | Follows from the sine addition formula (Real.sin_add) but the amplitude-phase form is not stated in Mathlib. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.amplitude_phase_form |
| CA-684 | For m x'' + b x' + k x = 0 with b^2 > 4 m k (overdamped), the solution is a combination of two decaying real exponentials with no oscillation. | 3:§7.3 Case 1 overdamped p.780 · 3:Example 7.19 p.781 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.overdamped | Special case of the distinct-real-roots solution. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.overdamped_spring |
| CA-685 | For b^2 = 4 m k (critically damped) the solution is e^(r t)(c1 + c2 t) with r < 0, non-oscillating. | 3:§7.3 Case 2 critically damped p.782 · 3:Example 7.20 p.782 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.critically_damped | Special case of the repeated-root solution. / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.critically_damped_spring |
| CA-686 | For b^2 < 4 m k (underdamped) the solution is a decaying exponential times a combination of sine and cosine, so it oscillates with decreasing amplitude. | 3:§7.3 Case 3 underdamped p.782 · 3:Example 7.21 p.783 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.underdamped, UndergradKB.Calculus.spring_solution_tendsto_zero | Special case of the complex-roots solution. The book also remarks that in all damped cases x(t) tends to 0 as t tends to infinity (same item). / SecondOrderODE.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.underdamped_spring |
| CA-687 | For a forced damped system m x'' + b x' + k x = f(t), the solution is a transient part (complementary solution, tending to 0) plus a steady-state part (a particular solution) governing long-term behavior; the natural frequency of the undamped system is w/(2 pi). | 3:§7.3 Forced vibrations (Eq. 7.8, transient and steady-state solutions) p.787 · 3:Example 7.23 p.787 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.forced_vibration_transient | Consequence of the nonhomogeneous general-solution theorem. / SecondOrderODE.lean: two solutions of the forced equation differ by a term tending to 0 (m, b, k > 0). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.forced_vibration_transient_steady_state |
| CA-688 | The charge q on the capacitor of a series RLC circuit satisfies L q'' + R q' + q/C = E(t). | 3:§7.3 Equation 7.9 (RLC series circuit) p.789 · 3:Example 7.24 p.790 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.rlc_iff_forcedSpring, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.rlc_solutions_differ_by_transient | Physical model; same mathematics as the forced spring-mass equation. / ODEExtras.lean. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.rlc_series_circuit |
| CA-689 | Power series method: assume y = sum a_n x^n, differentiate term by term, substitute, re-index, equate coefficients of each power to get recursion for the a_n. | 3:§7.4 Strategy: Finding Power Series Solutions p.793 · 3:Example 7.25 p.793 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.power_series_method, UndergradKB.Calculus.ODEExtras.power_series_oscillator, HasFPowerSeriesAt.hasDerivAt | Only the derivative of the sum at the centre (first coefficient) is checked in Mathlib; term-by-term differentiation at all points and the ODE method itself are missing. / ODEExtras.lean: coefficient recursion ⇒ the series (infinite radius) solves y'' + βy' + γy = 0. / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.power_series_solution_method |
| CA-690 | The Bessel equation of order 0, x^2 y'' + x y' + x^2 y = 0, has a power series solution sum (-1)^n x^(2n) / (4^n (n!)^2) (up to constant multiple). | 3:Example 7.26 p.797 | proved here | UndergradKB.Calculus.J0_bessel_equation, Complex.besselJ | Mathlib defines the Bessel function of the first kind (J_0 is this series) but the file lists its differential equation as a TODO, so the ODE statement is absent. / Bessel.lean: the series is twice differentiable and solves the equation on ℝ (term-by-term differentiation). / course layer (this library): UndergradKB.Calculus.Course.V3C7.bessel_equation_order_zero_series_solution |
